===== 0002601_18931230_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
"CAPITAL" CHAT.

The proposal to erects Jewish Toynbee Ball in the East End has now practically been accepted by the community. The building will in all probabil- ity be known as the Rothschild Hall, and will no doubt be eventually the centre of much valuable eacial and educational work.

I hear that the War Office authorities have just satisfactorily completed a series of important ex- periments in connection,with aerial navigation. The French have lately boasted, and • not without reason, of the advance they have made in connec- tion with their navigable balloons. I learn that the English War Office has quite as much reason for congratulation. The English experiments have been conducted in secrecy, but I have reason to believe that considerable progress has been made towards the solution of the chief difficulty—that connected with the provision of a reliable steering apparatus. ,

Although at the present time there appears very little likelihood of any skating, the National Skat- ing Association is busily preparing for the advent of the frost for which the devotees of the pastime are longing. Branches of the Association are being organized in various parts of the country, and in return for a small capitation fee the central body supplies copies of rules, tests, Stc., and the badges for speed or figure skating which may be gained by members. The metropolitan branch of the Associa- tion covers an area of twenty miles from Charing- cross, and candidates for the figure tests will have little trouble to find an examiner, some twenty-five judges having been appointed. Hendon and Wim- bledon are the headquarters for judging, but judges will attend at several other popular resorts. The races over various distdnces will also .be held at Hendon. The enthusiasts need not yet despair, for the first race last winter was not decided until the middle of January, •

The Parisians, true to their reputation, are mak- ing haste to laugh at the bombshell explosion at the Chamber of Deputies. Menufacturers of the dainty little trifles called " articles de Paris " have flooded the jewellers and fancy-goods shops this Christmas-tide with Vaillant's saucepans. These are miniature saucepans, set with paste diamonds and pearls, for ornaments. At the toyshops there is a new toy, the bomb which opens at the top. Directly the .lid is taken off, a little black devil darts out and ' makes a squeaking noise. Meanwhile, people who are in want of iron saucepans are delaying pur- chasing them as as long as possible. A work- ing-man who went into a large haztar and inquired the price of one was asked to wait it little while, and the assistant, having sent for a policeman, :.romptly arrested the would-be purchaser on sus- picion of being an Anarchist. Even the sprightly blies Lottie Collins did not escape these gruesome attentions. While in America she read her death notice in an English paper and sent back word that "a livelier corpse never kicked." The explanation of the mistake by, the "Times," that Sir George Berkeley who had really died had not received his title in time to be included in this year's knightage, recalls a similar experience of the "Morning Post." Two years ago that journal announced that Lady Blanche limier had died, forgetting that vol. Mr. Pet lift was born in London. and was descended from a Huguenot family, who fled from France and aftei - wards settled in Essex. Family misfortunes ham- pered his start in life, and when a boy of fifteen he was playing a small Irish part in a Sadler's Wells pantomime. He then commenced to send shorr. contributions to boys' periodical., and goon after took to teaching, at which he remained during six years.

Henry Pettitt's tendency towards dramatic work received its first practical stimulus when he met Paul Neritt. The two aspiring youths combined tAs. write a play for George Conquest at the Grecian. The play succeeded so well that young Pettitt left the school-room for good, became treasurer at the Grecian, collaborated with his employer in "Queen's Evidence," and floated to tortunn on the tide ot po?ularity. He has been associated in the produc- tion of play after play during the lass thirteen years, sometimes in conjunction with his old friend , Paul Meritt, at other times with Sir Augustu4, Harris, and nore recently with Mr. George R. Sims. He was a tall, spare-looking man with a ;pleasant open lace, tair hair, and a heavy drooping moustache which a trooper aright envy. He wen.", in fact, in every respect more kke a soldier than a literary man.

Sir George Elliot, whose death is announced, was known in his native county of Durham as the bon- nie pit laddie. He began life as a collier bey, even- tually developing into a working pitman. It is on record that be laid She foundatiam of Ms fortune by utilising the waste coal dust of the local mines in ; the manufacture of coke, thus inaugurating what is now one of the most profitable industciel of Dur- ham. Like most men who have made huge 8111318 by speculations in concessions, Sir George Elliot was a very genial man. His friends said his fingers had the virtues of Midas. He was recently engaged in many commercial speculations, especially in the South Welsh district and was supposed to be a Man of enormous wealth. A few months ago, when the coal strike was at its height, Sir George attracted ' widespread attention by his scheme that the coal- ownersshould amalgamate their interests in a co- operative company,charged with the working of the entire coal deposits of the country. He was among other things,head of one of the foremost wire manu- facturing firms in the kingdom,

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 30, 1893. LADIES' LETTER.

• .p accordance with other facts of moral origin and highest dictation. It is sometimes asserted that_ when people

live together, or are intimately associated, they- giow like each other, and we know that schcolboys are apt to catch any peculiarity of habit or expression of their tutor or schoolmaster. This is undoubtedly the case, but it is a very different thing from heredity. Physical peculiarities acquired accidentally are not transmitted. A man loses a leg, but his children are born with their proper compliment. For generations past it has been customary to cut off the ears and tails of certain breeds , of dogs, but it has not resulted in the establishment of a race of animals unfurnished with these useful appen- dages. On the other hand, when by a curious freak of nature a man is born with a supernumerary finger or toe he may transmit this peculiarity to his children. It sometimes happens that children of one sex exhibit an hereditary taint, whirst those of the opposite sex escape it. The boys "take after" the father and the girls after the mother, and a tendency to disease may be more or less powerful as the child resembles one or other parent. " The Family Physician."

Society received handsome Rutledge Chester with open arms. lie had always been a prime favourite h the young ladies, and they ha a not forgotten him, it seemed, judging from the cards he received.

"If 1 wero a poet I might find words in which to describe her. As lam not, Ido not know how to begin.," replied the young captain. "She is simply the loveliest girl in the wide world. 1 only know that her hair seems to have caught the glory of the sonlight. It is gold, with a beautiful, natural ripkle. She could bear from afar off the shrill shriek of an approaching train, and the thought occurred to her that she would take it, no matter whence the directirm. It mattered little to her where she went. She pushed through the crowd in waiting toward the ticket office. "That young girl is ill," whispered a motherly looking woman to her husband, as Uldene passed them. "See, she is as white as death, and her dark eyes burn like flame." Uldene heard her, and a wild hysterical sob rose to her lips. 11l I What was an the illness in the world corm pared to what she was suffering? She stood quite irresolute, gazing helplessly around her. The voice of the officious ticket clerk broke in upon her confused musings, Where to, miss ?" he inquired, in a brisk, pleasant manner. -Uldene did not know herself. She muttered some inherent reply. "Did you say you wished to go to—" The shriek of the approaching train drowned his voice. Uldene did not catch the name of the place he had mentioned. Slie knew he was waiting ins• patiently for her answer. "Yes, that is where I want to go," she said. away. The clerk looked after her, telling himself he had never seen co beautiful a creature. When Uldene was seated in the car she found that she had purchased a through tick-et for Balti- more. CHAPTER XXVI. THE RIVAL LOVERS. The grand ball-room was crowded when Rutledge tuna his friend reached the villa. Mrs. Renwick had often invited the senator's handsome eon to the villa, but he had never yet accepted an invita- tion. When she saw him enter the ball-room with Captain Lansing, she was delighted. She received him with the greatest cordiality, and he could not help feeling just a little flatteied. He passed on, the captain by his side. "I should imagine, by the disconsolate expres- sion of your face, you do not see your charming in- amorata," said Rutledge. "No, she is not here," returned the captain, adding : " I shall take but little interest in the ball until she does make her appearance." Lovely girls passed them by, smiling their sweetest and challenging theni with their bright, roguish eyes; but the captain looked anxiously past them all toward the door. At length his face brightened up, "She is here," he said, with a thrill of delight. "Come, Rutledge, and I will present you to the queen of the ball." And both of the young men crossed the room together. "She is always surrounded by a throng of admirers," said the captain, impatiently. ' We shall be obliged to await our turn. I shall not be able to claim one waltz," he raid, noticing how quickly her tablet was being filled." "Courage," laughed Rutledge. "A faint heart never wins fair lady ;" but he would net have said that's few hours later. "I shall press forward and try my fate," replied the captain eagerly. "I shall ask her for one waltz with the hope that she may give me two." "I hope she will," returned Rutledge, smiling at his friend's earnestness. But one hour afterward he would not have expressed that hope. From his position he could not see the young girl's face for the pretty fan she held before it. A moment later, he was bowing low before her. "Miss Sefton," _he heard the captain say, nejrvously, "allow me to present you my friend, Mt Chester." Rutledge raised his eyes in the most intense sur- prise, his heart thrilling with a pleasure so keen it was almost pain. Surely it could not be—yes, it was—Verlie, The meeting with Rutledge was quite as much of a surprise and shock to Verne. She VMS the first to recover her comp 'sure, however. "Is it possible you did not know Mr. Chester is , a relative of mine—by marriage ?" she asked turn- ing to the astonished captain, when the greeting was over. "The young wife whom he lost by death nearly two years ago was my beloved sister Uldene." At that moment, much to the captain's infinite annoyance, Mrs. Renwick, his hostess, came up to claim him. "1 shall not leave you: until you have promised me a waltz," he said to Verlie, anticipating that that was the very favour Rutledge had in- tendqd to ask. "1 hare just one waltz left," said Verlie, smil- ing; "I shall be pleased to give it to you." And that smile quite disturbed &Wedge Cheater. Both gentlemen gazed for one instant into that bewitching girlish face, and they looked at each ether sharply. The captain bowed and turned away, and much against his will, followed his hostess from the spot. "Verlie,' said Rutledge, when he found himself alone with her," will you come to the conservatory? I have so much to say to you—so much to talk to you about." She placed her little hand on his arm, but he did not notice how that little gloved hand trembled. He did not know the valiant effort she made to still the loud throbbing of her heart. Verlie had told herself long since that she had forgotten him; that the love that had once filled ' her heart for him had (lied out; but now, as she stood face to face with him again, she realised thet her love for him was still warm in her heart, and the knowledge made poor Verlie doubly shy with him. ' As they stood among the flowers in the con- servatory, listening to the splash of the perfumed' water of the fountain, they talked of Uldene. It was a great surprise to Rutledge to hear that Verlie had never ceased writing each week to Uldene's old address, but the letters had never been answered. How was he to know that beautiful, guilty 1 Uldene had always torn these letters up, that the Sef tons might lose all trace of her That half hour by the fountain neither of them ever forgot in all the long years of bitter pain and ' sorrow that followed. They both returned to the ball-room with a new, strange thrill in their hearts. The captain came up at once to claim Verlie for the waltz she had womised him, and away they floated together to the dreamy measure of the sweet I dance music. The captain quite thought it was the pretty compliments which he was paying her that brought that unusual flush to her cheek and the unwonted , sparkle to her bright blue eyes. How was he to I know that she was recalling every look that had I crossed Rutledge Chester's handsome face as they ' stood together by the fountain, and every word he had uttered? "They made a very handsome couple—Verlie and the captain," whispered his hostess, confi- dently. "1 should not wonder if it woula end in a match." The careless words pierced Rutledge Chester's heart like the thrust of a keen blade. The care- lessly spoken words brought a strange, troubled ' thought to his heart. If the captain won Verlie Sefton for his bride, all the light and brightness of this world would be over for him. Ile had often persuaded himself into the ballet' that it had been simply admiration that had filled I his heart in that never-to-be-forgotten past for sweet, golden-haired Verlie; and that the love of his life had been given to Inane, and his heart lay_huried in her gray%

When rldene reached her destination, end found herself alone in the crowded streets, she felt more forlorn and desperate than ever.. She vi as tempted to cry out for help and pity. She was cold and hungry, and the thought occurred to her to find a quiet boarding-house where she eould get a cup of tea and rest, and think, looking the dark, dread futuro fully in tne. face. By inquiry she found just such a plaoa as she needed, and secured a room at once. She was 8o thoroughly exhausted she was not able to leave it for three days. When, at last, she was able to go down to the little meagre sitting-room—by courtesy called a parlour—she saw a paper.lying on the centre-table, and the startling head-lines of the first column caught her eye, and held her spell-bound. "That was a terrible accident," said the voluble landlady, pointing to the column. " About mid- way down the page it tells of the grief of a frantic young husband who found the body of his bride so terribly mutilated that he could never have recog- nised her if it had not been for her wrap and satchel. How I wept for poor Mr. Chester as I read it." A low, suppressed cry fell from Uldene's With shaking, cold hands he picked up the paper, and the first paragraph that met her eye was the graphic account of her own tragio death by the wrecking of the train. She read on with dazed eyes, how she had been buried in the family vault, and of the wild grief of her husband. Uldens read the aeocuut through a second time, and a third time, and as she sat there holding the paper in her hand, she made to herself a solemn vow. As Rutledge believed her dead, she would remain so to him while her poor life lasted. He should never know that she lived. Fate must have foreseen this when the young girl in the seat at the back of her had consented to take chat ge of her wrap and satohel. It was more bitter than death, for she loved him better than life itself ; but the curse, the doom that hung over her—this hapless daughter of a fated race—made it imperative for her to put the whole world between herself and the man she loved. Yes, she would drag out her weary life in soli- tude, and Rutledge Chester should believe that she slept in the old vault in the graveyard. She would watch over him from afar like a guardian angel ; but he should never know—never know the pitiful truth. As she read further down another sentence met ,her eye. After she had read it she threw up her hands and fell like one dead to the floor. These were the words she read : "On the day following the burial of his beautiful young bride, Rutledge Chester had suddenly closed up his business affairs and had gone abroad, none knew whither.. To a friend who had accompanied him on board the steamer he had said he might be gone from his native land for years—perhaps forever." "May Heaven forgive me for my wicked thoughts toward poor Uldene," she sobbed, "now that she is dead.' Long and bitterly Verne mourned for Uldene. Hereditary Di s ease. We know that features, form, frame, peculiarity of constitution susceptibility to certain agents, not to speak of character mental and moral, the passions and the intellect, are often derived from progenitors many steps upwards in the ancestral tree. Individually we are combinations of many ancestors. The actual traits of the parents may and may not be seen in their offspring, and it 18 more common to find that one or two only are represented in each child. The remainder are doubtless derived from some ancestor long forgotten, whose intellectual powers or defects, infirmities, or vigour of body, whose faults and follies, whose brilliant powers and miserable failings may he reflected in a remote descendant, as he himself has derived them from some distant ancestor. We are accustomed to say that gut may skip a generation, and why may it not skip four or five? Hereditary tendency is probably of far more remote origin'than is commonly supposed, and is a reflection of the tendencies of untold numbers who have preceded us in the family tree. It is a frightful thing thus to look back on the sins of our forefathers and to recognise the transmitted punishment, but it is its

Germs.

Germs will settle on ot-1-1:- body, on our food, in our drink. So much, at any rate, it is easy enough to see. That, however, they can enter it by the skin is a sug- gestion which can only be made to be at once put aside; but that they can enter through the lungs is a hint which cannot be so easily disposed of, and Professor Nageli, at any rate, is strongly of opinion that some germs at least are able, when they settle on the thin wall which sepasates the air in the lungs from the blood , which it sapplies, to set up on this wall an inflamma- tion, to separate thereby the tissues, and so to enter by that little cleft into the great nutrient current which circulates through the body. Just as certain, too, is it that the light germs taken in with the fluids that we drink are able, by some means to make their way into the general system of the body ; but to enter into a consideration of such diseases as fevers, erysipelas, or cancer, by whatever ways .these commence to operate on the body, would be a tiresome, if not a painful, task. One example will be sufficient to show wherein lies the essence of the ,whole matter. If you have some grapes, and leave them exposed to the air, they become dry, , unfit for eating, and are thrown away ; but if, instead of so leaving the grapes, you, without adding anything to them, break their skins by, for example, treading them as wine-makers of Italy still do, and then leave them, you will -find a very different result. Fermenta- tion will be set up, and the sweet conteets of the grape will give rise to carbonic acid gas and to alcohol. You have added nothing, and yet you got this result; the reason ought not now to be very hard to find. Surely enough, the germs of the plant that flourish in the rich 'contents of the grape-skin bag were deposited from the air on the skin ; they could not reach the juice till the skin was broken, but when that had been artifieially done, the germs and the juice came together, and the result, long ago known to mankind, of the formation of fresh bodies was brought about. It will be unnecessary to point out how, by somewhat similar processes, a liv- ing organism may be made the prey of the chemical changes brought about by oth,:r organisms living at its 'expense. Putting, then, asiele the belief that, in the present, life arises from anything else than pre-existing living matter, we find ourselves face to face with one 61 the grandest results of later times. The bread we eat, the wine we drink, the disease that weakens or kills us, the hospital fever which retards our recovery, are like ourselves, and like all animals and plants, "'Life's chil- dren." Go where we will, do what we may;theipowers of life are not only working within us but around us, shaping the fates of men and the fortunes of nations,and yet working always under just the same laws, obeying past the same principles, and causing always much the same effects; breaking up tissues and compounds, using up formed matter dependent on other matter, owing always their existence to destruction- elsewhere. Beautiful as life is represented to be, such is what life really is; for man it remains, and for man it is a privi- lege, to order the course of many of its processes. And among these, there is none with which he ought more firmly to battle, none that he ought to learn to conquer, more than these "Germs "which, while they serve him when lie makes them aid his will by assisting him in his arts and industries, do by their allies bring on him and on his food se many of the woes that flesh is heir to.-- Science for AU."

"THE MEMBER FOR INDIA."

ENTHUSIASTIC RECEPTION AT,LAHORE. L.kllolo3,TllllBDAT.—Mr.Dadabhai Naoroji,M. P. ,arrived here yesterday, and had a most enthusiastic reception. The approaches to the railway station were literally blocked oy the immense crowd, which cztended,to the distance of half a mile. On the platform were assembled the members of the Indian Congress Committee and the leaders of the Parses and Mahometan communi- ties. Addresses were presented from the Reception Committee, the Mahometan citizens, and students, after which a procession was formed and drove through the prtnetpat streets, the passage occupying four hours. Numerous triumphal arches had been erected along the route, and hqedreds ot flags and banners hung from the windows i many bearing appropriate mottoes, among which "Thanks to the Finsbury electors" was conspicu- ous. Nothing approaching this demonstration has ever before Nen witnessed in the Punjaub.

BY J. U. BRAKE.

I

111 E AMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 30, 1893.

rou aria; never; &W Tie,-1

with "iny lif will— I looked up. Jack, who personated the masked bandit, was bursting with laughter. . little lady, who had nestled close to my bre, also looked up with blushing confusion. Her ha, had become disordered by the roughness of my em- brace, and revealed the fact that her raven tresses were those of a fashionable wig, and, beneath tin .disguase of an Eastern Lady—l was bolding lit* Jessie Heath to my heart. The flood of happiness that overwhelmed me ati finding my shadowy love in the plump little body before me, destroyed any remains of prudence I had left, and I stooped and kissed her glowing cheeks. Of course, all this was very bad behaviour on my • part, but I question if any of my gentlemen readers would have behaved differently in the same situation.

Jack had discreetly retired, and, thus left to ourselves, I pleaded my love with all the powers of eloquence I possessed, and I wrung from her the admission tlrat, hearing me so often spoken of,' she had come prepared "to like me a little," but, she added, with charming naked 1 "You will be sorry you said so much. I Itta only a poor girl,—dependent on—" I interrupted her with the exclamation that "I was glad of it. K was a poor man but making good progress in my profession (a lover's terra- diddle), and, with her by "my side, I feared nothing,"—and then I told her .of my uncle's line of action, and how impossible it was tor me to en- tertain any such project, and he was at liberty to give me up altogether. "Your uncle has acceted the invitation and will be here this evening," she replied. "Had you not better see him, and tell him at once?" I expressed my intention to do so; and, although I was very much in earnest, I felt rather nervous at what I regarded as a turning point in my life. When we met I phenged into the subject at once. I told him I was quite unable to meet his wishes, as I was already engaged 'to the•dearest, the sweet- est. and best little girl in the world. "And, pray, may I ask," he returned, with grim irony, "What you have to offer in return to the sweetest and best little girl in the world ? " "Nothing, lam sorry to say. Nothing but my love, and my determination to work and surround her with every comfort that perseverance can obtain." .

"Ah 1 rather high fainting'; but, may Ibe in- formed the name of this sweet little girl ? " Miss Jessie Heath," I replied, proudly; :Instead of my uncle going into a towering pas- sion, and threatening to cut me off with a shilling and a silver snuff-box, he sat down in a chair and laughed till his sides shook with merriment. At length he said, half-choked with the effort: "Has this sweet little' lass any money?" "Not any, I am happy to say. The man who requires money with his wife is— " " Fiddle-laddlel How about the butcher's and baker's bills? Do you intend to live upon poetry and filberts? There,—there,—it is no use trying to teach young fellows sense who have lost their heads; but I should like to see this sweet girl' who has been able to make such a fool of my clever nephew." Without vouchsafing a xeply to this implied taunt, I went out and fetched Jessie.

To my astonishment, as -soon as she saw my uncle she went straight to him, and, putting her arms round his neck, kissed him on both cheeks. The old gentleman took very kindly to the action, and, holding herat .arm's,length in front of hitn, said': "Well, Walter, I think ion 'have a glimmering of sense after all,", and, seeing my looks of sur- prise and annoyance, he added, poking me in the ribs, "You need not look so glum. Jessie and I are old friends. ,Jessie is the liar° lass I have been saving up for you. So now there is nothing to pre- vent us spending a happy 'Christmas." I subsequently learnt that it had 'been a plot between my uncle and Jack's friends to bring me into Jessie s.company ; and, I also learned, that, after I had protested so strongly against the belief in ghosts, they had determined to play-me a trick. The bedroom I occupied was adjoining the room set apart for the theatrical performance, and P. small aperture, by means of a sliding panel, had been made for the purpose of ventilation between the two rooms. Through this opening, the light from a spectrescope had been thrown into my room, and, by means of sundry reflecting-glasses (things well known to most people, but which, from my early country life and subsequent isolated habits, I had never aeon), the sceqesibave described were produced. And now the question may be asked, "Am I still a disbeliever in ghostly visitations?" My answer is, most .decidedly "No." Looking into thee face of my little ghost—now sitting by my side,—l confess to the profoundest conversion. lam happy in my faith, and care little for the opinions of the outside world. 'Christmas may come, and Christmas may go—but, as long. es I live, I will never:give up the ghost.

A DREADFUL- NIGH

Seen a ghost? No, and I don't want. Ghosts are not in my line. I prefer something more solid. But wasn't I once terribly scared? Oh, .yes ;sand I'll tell you all about it.• It is about twenty years ago that I, then but a stripling of sixteen years ord, was spending my Christmas vacation sa my uncle's place in Shrop- shire. Uncle John was still but a young man, and a bachelor, his mother keeping house for him. But ' be was of great importance in my eyes, owing to his being the captain of the local cricket club; which was one of more than merely local importance, and ,played an annual two days' match with the „givers of cricket, the famous Marylebone Cricket Xlub. Hence, schoolboy-like, I held him in great awc, and when, on the fourth day of my arrival, which was Christmas Eve, he said to me, " Look •here, Freddy, we shall have a lot of people arrive •to-day, and as we are crowded for room, and your ,getting up early to go skating would disturb the ,people who will sleep along the same corridor &gyms .have been doing, you'll have to shift downstairs 11 Ithe room off the billiard-room" I didn't dare IA iutter a word of remonstrance. But none the less was I greatly troubled in my ' mind • for in this room, in my grandfather's time, one of the footmen used to sleep, and one night burglars had forced the window which opened on the lawn, had found him in bed, and had smothered him with the pillow. Add to these undoubted facts the other one that all the servant firmly believed 'that the ghost of poor Thomas 8611 haunted the room, that they told me so when I asked them to shift.my luggage there, and you can easily believe how the rest of that afternoon was embittered by the thought of the night that was to follow. Well, about ten o'clock p.m. I found myself in bed, and also in. a ea/c/ bath of perspiration, with my eyes tightly closed, and .endeavouring to go to sleep before the adukia of the house should have re. tired. After a time I did deep. But, alas, it was only for a time, and the half clock awoke me by striking twelve. Horror ! Why, that was the hour " when ghosts do walk" flashed upon my brain, and I trem- bled in every limb. The Moon was shining through the openings in the shutters, and throwing all kinde of queer shadows about the room. A large prese, which stoo& in one corner, and which I had never looked into, began to harass me with anxiety as to its contents. I slipped cautiously out .e bed, and approached the press and with a curios's mixture of fear and ,defiance, 'I flung it open. It a-as empty. Back I went to bed, and had just curled myself up. beneath the clothes, when I thought I heard re. inrati ens from tinder the mattress—l heard groans 'I began to feel the mattress move Under me. ." No, dash it all !" cried I; "I'm not going to be frightened to death like this, and for nothing." The moon was skirting brightly into the room es I leaped out of bed, and lifted the valance. I was in bed again quicker than a flash of light- ning, and curled up like a ball in the blankets. A burglar I could have faced, a ghost I bed half expeea fed, bat 'a CO.g7L—au enormous coffin—l was quite unprepared for. But there it was, sure enough, and no doubt with a murdered man inside it. For six mortal hours I lay there trembling, and as soon as it was daylight I bopped out of bed, and attired in my night-gown alone, tore upstairs to my uncle's room and woke him up. "Uncle, undo 1" said I; there's been a murder in the house, and they've put the coffin under my bed !" " Eh, what?" said he, only half awake. Then, bursting into e. fit of laughter, he exclaimed, "Why, you little fool, what are you talking about? Glo back to bed ugairi. That's only the long box in which we keep our practice nets. Couldn't you see A.a.c, on the side ?"

NEW YEAR BUSINESS CHANGES.

Preliminary Announcement RESPECTIIiG THE

"LANCASTER" CALF FOOD,

ALTHOUGH the " LANCASTER " CALF FOOD has attained a popu- larity which we believe is second to none, we have found it necessary, in order to make it a commercial success, to associate our- -selves with some firm manufacturing under the most economic conditions possible. We know of no other firm so favourably located or so well equipped for the work as Messrs. J. BIBBY and SONS, the well- known Feeding Experts of Liverpool, and we have pleasure to announce that we have made an arrangement with them to manu- facture for us after Ist JANUARY, 1891.

Altogether, apart from the wido tech- nical knowledge they possess of this partic- ular business, Messrs. J. BIBBY and SONS fortunately have probably the best located, the best equipped, and the largest factory in the world for the manufacture of FEEDING CAKES and MEALS.

We may state, that although we have sold thousands of tons of the "LANCAS- TER "CALF FOOD and the " LANCAS- TER " CATTLE FOOD, we can scarcely remember to have had a complaint. We do not think our record in this respect can be beaten, and we have, therefore, arranged that these two MEALS shall be made on the formula we have followed for so many years, and they will be sent out EXACTLY OF THE SAME COMPOSITION AND IN THE SAME PACKAGES AS HERE- TO-FORE.

On the other hand, in tho manufactur- ing of rour COMPOUND CAKE we have availed ourselves very largely of the wide information and technical skill of Messrs. J. BIBBY and SONS. and the "CARRU- THERS "CAKE and' CARRUTHERS " MEAL will in consequence possess superior merit to anything wo have ourselves been able to produce. Under the now arrangement our custom- ers will in most cases—after the NEW YEAR—be able to obtain their supplies from local merchants and this will no doubt greatly facilitate business to the advanta,go of all concerned.

We may only add that Measrs. J. BIBBY and SONS' full address, to whom 01 com- munications and correspondence should be addressed after the Ist JANUARY next, is_ J. BIBBY & SONS, EXCHANGE CHAMBERS, LIVERPOOL We are, Your Obedient Servants,

CARRUTHERS & CO., LANCASTER.

CANNIBALISM IN THE PACIFIC. -- LANDING OF BRITISH AND FRENCH BLUE JACKETS. _ _ .

_ , A non-commissioned officer on board Her Majesty's ship Boomerang, torpedo gunboat, one of the seven vessels forming the Colonial Defence Squadron, writing Sydney on November 8, gives a graphic account of froth several exciting incidents in the Boomerang's recent cruise among the islands of the Wester's Pacific. At Noumea reports reached the Boomerang of a case of can- nibalism and two of plunder by natives. . At Villa, in the Sandwich Islands, they heard of the massacre of a boat's crew of the American schooner Don Henri at Pentecost Island. Captain Bruce, of the American vessel, stated that early in September, calling at Pentecost Island to engage natives for work in the plantations, he sent off a , boat containing Id. Litton. a French trader, and three natives. When they reached the shore they were at- tacked by savages armed with tomahawks, and M. Lit- ton and two of his boat's crew were murdered. They subsequently found the bodies of N. Litton and one native lying on the beach terribly mutilated. Be- fore the Boomerang had time to investigate this murder, another report came to hand of the massacre of a boat's crew of a French trading _schooner, ,the Neptune, at Aurora Island. The Boomerang immediately left for Aurora Island, where she fell in with the French warstip Scorff. She, too, had heard of the murders. Th2re exists a conven- tion between Great Britain and France with regard to these islands for the protection of life and property and the suppression of the kidnapping trade. The warships determined to punish the Pentecost Islanders for the as- sassination of the beat's crew of the:American steamer, and proceeded thither. On their arrival the Boomerang and Scorff each landed 48 armed blue-jackets at differ- ent points, the French being more to the north, with in- structions to converge on a particular village where the murderers were known to reside. Ten natives of the Loyalty Island and four Pentecost Islanders accompanied each party as guides. On reaching—the village the British force was to await a. slgnal from the French on which both parties spread out arid several of the mur- derers Were captured. The French force' wandered as- tray and did not get up in time to co-operate except to assist in burning the village. The Boomerang and See& then sailed tei Aurora Islaud, where parties were landed and similar tactics followed. The French party shot one of the murderers who was trying to escape, and another was captured. Two villages were destroyed and fines inflicted on others. The captives from Pentecost and Aurora Islands were tried, found guilty of murder, and sentenced to im- prieonment for life at the French conVict settlement at Noumea. Whsn the Pentecost native was made ac- (painted with his sentence, he died almost immediately. At Pentecost Island upwards of twenty white men have been massacred and robbed; but never before have ' strong measures been taken against the natives. At , several of the islands the natives are cannibals. At Pen- tecost, for instance, large quantities of human bones were found, probably belonging to the crew of the Don Henri, a ease of cannibalism was also reported at Malo Island.

HOW CHRISTMAS WAS SPENT

AT WARRINGTON.

Another Christmas has passed away; and with it have disappeared tor another year the festivities and feastings which come and go with the sacred day. Despite the climatic eccentricities of the pre- vious week,there were those who clung tenaciously to the hope that " an old fashioned Christmas" would at the eleventh hour envelop us in its chilly embrace. But such hopes were destined to remain unfulfilled. "Father " Christmas was inexorable and continued to coquet with all the seasons and never once appeared in his silvery apparel. On Christmas Day, indeed, his conduct was quite distressing. In the morning people were allured into wearing spring costumes, and thinking pleasantly on the verdancy of May. But lo ! in the after- noon there came a sudden change. The bright warm sun disappeared ; the sky grew sullen and black. A mournful wind began to whisper and wail about the houses when people were enjoying their Christmas dinner —that particular meal of all the year. Then the gusts grew stronger and became more erratic and tumultuous, and finally, on the back of a shrieking gale, Jupiter Dluvius rushed upon the town, and with spluttering splashing rage beat upon everything that was exposed with drenching violence. And then what a miserable five hours ensued! The May like weather of the morn- ing was supplanted by wintery blasts of north-west wind, laden with showers of hail and sleet. The streets were all deserted, and people were glad to crowd closer round the blazing fires, and allow chilly shivers to creep along their backs at the bare thought of being out of doors. But again a change occurred. A lull followed , thesterm. The rain gradually ceased, then the wind ' dropped its fierce howlings and fell to low complainiags with an occasional wail and sob, and at last degenerated into a contented gentle breeze. The moon began to reveal itself from behind the fleeting clouds, and soon the night was fair and mild. The streets were soon alive again with people and bands of carollers, who kept up their somewhat questionable rejoicings until the early hours of the morning, just as they did on Christnoaseve. The day itself was not spent like an ideal Christmas Day, except so far as the recognition of religious obligations went and the continuance of charitable institutions were concerned. Special services were of course held in the various places of worship.

===== 0002601_18931230_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR'S HOT POT.

At noon theMavor (Alderman Green&ll) provided the usual hot pot for the poor. For this atteut 600 tickets had been distributed to deserving families, aid from 11 o'clock until two, the distributors had a busy time sup- plying the food. It might also be mentioned that in ad- dition to the hot pot, each child was presented with a mince pie. The hut pot was prepared by Mr. Powys, the caretaker of the Town Hall. Amongst those present at the Drill Hall were Alderman Wright, Councillor Smethurst, Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), Mr. Star.- ley Wilson (deputy town clerk), Mr. John Faircleugh, Miss Bessie Dutton,Mr. John Chidlaw,Mr. Peter Chrimes, Mr. H. Toft, Mr. liorsfield, and Mrs. W.J. Forshaw. The Mayor and Mayoress were away at Tenbv.

AT THE WORKHOUSE.

The usual custom of giving the inmates of the Work- house a Christmas dinner of roast beef and plum pudd- ing was carried out in the large dining hall at noon. The room had been decorated witk evergreens and flowers. The following gentlemen were present i. Bev. H. V. Pigot (chaplain), Alderman Francernb (deputy mayor), Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs. Alfred Jolley, Shaw and Hill (guardians), Mr. Samuel Sutton (assistant clerk to the Board), Mr. Frank Aukland (relieving officer), Dr. Peacockc, and Messrs. Atherton, David Walton,Thorrias Rose, George Holt, and Thomas Mason, Mr. and Mrs. Ward (rnakter and matron), were in attendance, and at- tended to the wants of the inmates, assisted by several of the visitors as well as by the following officials : Miss Ward, Mr. Dobson, Mrs. Langley (assistant matron), Nurses Lohlev and Smale, Mr.and the Misses Curry. Pre- vious to the dinner, a service was held in the dining ball, conducted by the Rev. T. M. R. Young Hughes,curate at St. Paul's Church. The dinner was heartily enjoyed by the inmates,each man receiving an allowance of one pint of beer, and the wemen half a pint. The children were . supplied with coffee, and each one received a present in ' addition. At the conclusion Mr. Ward, the master, ac- knowledged several gifts he had received. The Mayor , and Mayoress had sent tobacco and pipes for the men, tea and sugar for the women, and oranges and crackers , for the children. Sir Gilbert Greenall, Mr. John Cros- field, and Mr. T. H. Lyon supplied the evergreens ; Miss Broadbent sent crackers ; Mrs.A. Dakin, Latchford, toys ; ,Miss Dakin, Christmas cards ; and Mr. Samuel Sutton, box of sweets. Three cheers were given for the Board of Guardians. Mr. Shaw and Mr. Hill responded, the latter remarking that the money which had pur- chased the dinner did not belong to the Guardians, but they had the disposal of it. At this point in the pro- ceedings one of the inmates named George Gee favoured the audience with a reading of one of his own poetical effusions on the event. Two of the verses arc as fol- lows : Poverty Hall once more is gay, The inmates spend their Christmas Day In style as did their sires of yore, With food substantial in galore. The worthy Guardiaqs, ever kind, The poor this day have held in mind, Filled many a wounded heart with joy Reminding them of days gone by. Alderman Francomb who next spoke said that he could not understand a man possessing so much poetical ability finding his way to the workhouse. He must be like the I little flower that was allowed to bloom on some desert land where no one could see it. There were few people ,in Warrington who could write such poetry as that to I which they had just listened. Ile noticed that Gee called the Workhouse " Poverty Hall." , Perhaps that was an appropriate name, but he thought if he called it " Con- ' venience Hall "it would be much better, because they knew that it was difficult for certain workingmen and I women to provide for their old age ; and when strength had left them they had to retire to that building. He was sure that the Master and Matron and Miss Ward, ' and all the other officials did everything in their power to make the inmates happy and comfortable. (Hear, , hear, and applause.) It was, he felt sure, the wish of I every ratepayer that the inmates should be treated 93 well and as fairly as it was possible to treat them.—The ' Rev. H. V. Pigot, as chaplain of the Workhouse, also spoke, and wished the inmates a Merry Christmas, and a Happy New Year when it came. They were indebted to the Guardians for the dinner they had just had, and although, as Mr, Hill had just said, the money did not come out of their pockets altogether, they could be churlish if they chose, and make Christmas Day a dark and dreary one to them, instead of a day full of bright- ness. Re begged to propose a vote of thanks to the officials for preparing the dinner. He had had nothing to do with the work himself, but he was sure that to those who had prepared it, it had been a labour of love. (Applause.) The motion was carried by acclamation. ,In the evening an entertainment was given.

===== 0002601_18931230_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PADGATE INDUSTRIAL SCHOOLS.

At the Padgate Industrial Schools, the children were treated with the same sumptuous fare as at the War- rington Workhouse on Christmas Day. The prepara- tions for the dinner of roast beef and plum pudding were carried out by Mr. and Mrs. Mines. There were present Mr. William Houghton, Mr. E. Aukland, Rev. J. J. Langharn (chaplain), and Mrs. Langham. After the removal of the cloth, Mr. Mines addressed the children, wishing them a Merry Christmas, remarking that the children of that school were as well cared for as the children of any similar institution in the country. Their lirst duty was to thank the Guardians for their great consideration in providing such a. sumptuous repast for them. The Guardians were very anxious about their welfare.; they took'a great interest in them, and they saw that they wanted for nothing. They must never forget the kindness that had been shown to them in that school, and be hoped that they would show their appre- ' ciationof it as they grew up, by, their conduct. Three cheers were then given for the Guardians and for the officers of the Union. After Mr. William Houghton had addressed the children, the Rev. J. J. Langham, in wish- ing the children and officers a happy Christmas, ex- pressed a hope that the happiness they experienced that day they would carry with them throughout the year. He pointed out how very much they themselves could do to make one another's lives happy by doing little acts of kindness one to another when they had the opportu- nity. He then called for three hearty oheers for Mr. and Mrs.Mines,which were given lustily. Cheers were also given for Mr. Aukland, and the children dispersed. Dur- ing the proceedings the school band played several pieces in a creditable manner. The rest of the afternoon was spent hy the children in games of various kinds, and in the evening an entertainment was held. The Mayor and Mayoress sent a present of oranges and crackers for the' children. , AT THE INFIRMARY.

The patients at the Warrington Infirmary were on ehristmas Day provided with an excellent dinner, and in the evening tile contents of a Christmas tree containing pipes and tobacco, Sze., for the men, presents for the women,and toys for the children, were distributed, Dr. Langham, Miss Watkiris (matron), and several invited friends including Councillor Smethurst were present. The respective wards looked very bright and cheerful, being well warmed and lighted, and ornamented with flowers and evergreens. The nurses and visitors went from bed to bed conversing with and cheering the pati- ents, some of whom were in a state of convL lesence,and it was evident that the effort thus made to enable them to pass a happy hour was much appreciated. Each patient received a present of some kind, and the Mayoress had also forwarded a liberal supply of clothing. 1 Messrs. Milling mad Co., Limited, supplied sweets and oranges, Mr. U. Toft, tobacce, Mrs. Silcock, Museum- street, fruit, and Mrs. John Crosfield the evergreens used in the decoration of the respective rooms. In the after- noon the Rev. 11. V. Pigot and Mrs. Pigot and their chil- dren, accompanied by several members of St. Paul's Church choir, visited the institution, and sang appropri- ate hymns and carols, the Misses Pigot bringing with them a Supply a Christmas cards and other presents.

===== 0002601_18931230_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
REJOICINGS_OF "THOMAS ATKINS."

Christmas Day at Orford Barracks was spent in the usual Christmas fashion. After attending divine service in the morningon their return the men sat down to a most sumptuous dinner, including turkeys, geese, pluin pudd- ing, beer, wines and spirits also being provided. The rooms of the barracks were most artistically decorated with coloured paper, fairy lamps, ivy and holly, inter- spersed with a few mottoes. A very pretty effect was produced, and the work was done by the non-commis- ' sioned officers. A large number of outsiders visited the Barracks during the afternoon and evening. Dancing was indulged in until the wee' sma' hours of Tuesday morning, although the rooms were so crowded as to render dancing almost impossible. _

===== 0002601_18931230_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
AT THE CHURCHES.

The special Christmas service in Wycliffe Church on Sunday evening was large attended. Selections from the " Messiah" were given by the choir and a small band of instrumentalists. Solos were rendered by MM. R. H. Pearson, Messrs. A. Griffiths, W. H. Wallington, and Scattergood. Miss Roberts was unable to be present, and in the solo "lie shall feed his flock," which she was to have sung, Mr. Scattergood sang in her stead. The instrumentalists were Messrs. R. H. Pearson, J. H. Knowles. J. Burrows, 11. Bond, R. Whitfield, and Char- wick. Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ. Appro- priate hymns were sung and the Rev. John Yonge de- livered a brief address, wishing every member of the congregation a happy Christmas. The real happiness of Christmas, he said, was religious happiness. No reason could be urged against a feast of good things if they had rightly come by them, and partook of them in moderation. Christian principles should govern them in everything, and most pre-eminently in a festival that had the celebration of the birth of Christ Iliinself as its origin and aim. There should be no unseemly extravag- ance or indulgence, and Christmas festivities should be governed by a spirit of unselfishness, each one caring for the rest, seeking to serve and benefit the rest. Nothing should be done that would dishonour Christ, the Master of the feast. There should be a universal burying of hatchets on Christmas Day, and the quarrels of 1893 should not go over into the year 1894. A collection was taken in aid of the choir fund, and realised over £7. A short service was held on Christmas Day in Cairo- st. Unitarian Chapel. The Rev. W. H. Drummond, pas- tor,preached an excellent sermon appropriate to the sea- son. The service was very well attended. Special hymns land carols were sung,and an anthem was effectively ren- dered by the choir, under the direction of Mr.J.M. Muir, A.C.O.—The annual tea party and ball in connection with the Sunday school was held in the schoolroom, Cairo-st., on Tuesday evening. About 300 'persons sat down to tea, the tables being presided over by the teachers. The entertainment afterwards held, consisted of a magic lantern, manipulated by Councillor Monks, the descriptions being given by the Rev. W. H. Drum- mond. Dancing was afterwards indulged in to music supplied by Mr. Ford. At St. Mary's Roman Catholic Church. Buttermarket- st., the festival of Christmas was celebrated. At half- past six o'clock on Christmas morning the first low mass was said, and every half hour until ten o'clock masses , were offered up. At eleven o'clock solemn high mass commenced, Father Wray officiating, assisted by Father Sumner, rector of St. Mary's, as deacon ; and Father Duggan,sub-deacon. During the service Father Sumner on behalf of the clergy of St. Mary's, wished the congre- gation all the joys of the festive season. The music of the mass was Haydn's Grand Mass No. 2, which was given in an excellent manner, Mr. Richard Eccles con- ducting, and Mr. T.Mather being at the organ. During the offertory the " Adeste Fideles " was sung. The crib or representation of the Saviour's birth, was placed in the side porch, on the east side of the church. Figures of St. Joseph and Mary were to be seen kneeling, with a figure of the Infant Saviour lying on straw in the centre of a cavern stable, and heads of the ox and the ass were placed at the back. In the evening of Christmas Day Solemn Fenediction of the Blessed Sacrament was given at halt-past six o'clock by Father Wray, 0.5.8., assisted by the Rector, Father Sumner, 0.5.8., as deacon, and Father Duggan, 0.5.8., sub-deacon. The church Was decorated with ivy, holly, &c., and in the chancel and side chapel of the Sacred Heart and Lady Chapel, the banners belonging to the different gait& were displayed.

===== 0002601_18931230_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

WEDNESDAY.

The House of Commons re-assembled on Wednesday after the Christmas holidays. Many questions were en the paper. Replying to one relative to certain threaten- ing utterances at a recent meeting of unemployed on Tower Hill, the HOME SECIIETARY said that although violent and foolish language had been used, the speakers so qualified their words that it would be difficult, if not impossible, to convict them. At the same time the Government were determined to set the law iu motion against persons who used language inciting to crime. Mr. MIINDELLA, in answer to Mr. Tomlinson, said it would be premature to state what course the Govern- ment intended to take with regard to the report on rail- way rates. Mr. GLansroNn, who was loudly cheered on entering the House, replied to some' of the questions. Be informed Mr. Chamberlain that Sir Gerald Portal's report on Uganda was in the hands of the Government ; but the situation was so complex, there belt'e other parties on the ground, that lie was unable to state when the question was likely to be brought before • the House. Mr. LEESE took his seat on re-election for Accrington, and was heartily cheered. The House went into Committee on the Local Govern- ment Bill and resumed consideration of clause 19, which deals with the election of Guardians. Mr. TOMLINSON. for Mr. WHITELEY, moved an amendment giving power to a municipal borough to elect annually a number of representatives on the Board of Guardians of any union in which the borough was situated. The amendment was rejected on a division by 122 votes to 70. Two other amendments were rejected, after some obstructive tactics, and the House adjourned at half-past five o'clock.

===== 0002601_18931230_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
THURSDAY

In the Mouse of Commons on Thursday evening Sir U. KAY-SitUrFLEWOUTII gave an account of the behaviour of Her Majesty's ship Resolution in the recent gale in the Bay of Biscay. Ile said that he had received a report that the damage she had sustained would be repaired in a few days at a cost of about £350, and that there was no question as to the stability of the ship. Mr. BUXTON, in answer to a question, said the Colonial Officer had received no information from Sir H. Loch as to the rumoured British reverse in Matabeleland. The House again went into Commitee on the Local Government (Parish Councils) Bill.

THE BURIALS QUESTION AT ASHTON-

IN-MAICERFIELD.

' In the House of Commons on Thursday evening, Mr. CALtcELT %V ILLIaMS asked the Home Secretary whether he was aware that the Vicar of St. Thomas's Ashton-in- Makerfield, on learning that a Nonconformist burial service was desired, refused to permit the interment in the new graveyard of that parish of Mrs. Merry, a par- ishioner,on the ground that Nonconformists had objected to the provision of such graveyard, and would grant a grave space in the old churchyard only, and that the Rector of Holy Trinity, Downall Green, in the same township, refused to grant new graves when the Church ef England service was not to be performed; and whether he would take steps to protect the parishioners of 'those two parishes in the exercise of their parochial rights, that the intention of the Legislature in passing the Burials Act of 1880 might not he defeated. Mr. AsQUITH: I am imformed by the Vicar of St. Thomas's that he did not refuse to permit the interment of Mrs. Merry in the new graveyard of the parish, that Mr. Merry had the choice of a grave either in the old or new ground, and that he selected the old became his relations had two graves there which were not then filled up. As to the Rector of Holy Trinity, I may remind icy hon. friend that he put the same question to me on the 25th July last. I then told him that I was satisfied that the rector claimed and exercised the right of appropriat- ing private graves to members of his own congregation and of leaving Nonconformists to be buried in graves where one or more interments had already taken place, and he sought to justify his action on the ground of the limited space available for new graves in the church- yard. The Rector now informs :roe that he is not aware that any new case has occurred since that time, and as he refers to the explanation he then gave I conclude that his practice is still the same. I can only repeat my statement, that I was not prepared to say that the Rec- tor was not acting within his legal rights,but I must ex- press my strong opinion that such a discrimination as he seeks to exercise is in entire variance with the intention and spirit of the Burials Act, 1830.

In the course of an inquiry on Tuesday at to the death of an infant who had been suffocated in bed, Dr. Dan- ford Thomas, coroner for Central Lendon. said he had an average of one such case before him every working day of the year—fully 300 cases a year,—all of which might be prevented by the use of a cot placed by the bedside, a practice which in Germany was enforced by law.

ALSA.. ALSAM ALSAM ALSAM LsAy ,LSAr MINE IN THE WORLD.

NOTES.

- - The efforts that are being made in many dis- tricts to avert the introduction of a School Board are not likely to be successful in every instance. A number of suggestions are made as being sufficiently strong to oveicome the imme,iate necessity of a Scho-1 Board, such as the payment of a voluntary subscription by the parents of the children attending school, or the payment of a voluntary rate by the ratepayere. It is due to the activity of the present , vice-President of the Education Department that many denominational managers find it exceedingly difficult to comply with the conditions of the Act of 1891, consequengy they are beginning to cast about for assistance in the ways indicated above. A movement of this character is now being set going in Earlestown and the ratepayers have been fav- oured with a circular asking their acceptance of a voluntary rate of 4d. in the X to meet current loss and from the nucleus of a fund out of which to pro- vide aelditional school accommodation.

It is to be hoped that the ratepayers will resist this sectarian move, and decline either to pay a, subscription or a voluntary rate under the present circumstances. And why should this be done? It is well known that in Earlestown, elementary edu- cation is in the hands of strict denominationalists; and it is'not so much a question of an increase in the rates that is feared, as the future of the present schools. It is seen that if a School Board is intro- duced, voluntary schools can never hope to compete with the schools under the management of the Board, with the result that they will either have to be handed over to the Board, or else given up. But a far more disastrous result in their eyes would be the loss of power over the young children in the matter of religious education. The opportunity would be lost for teaching sect:rian creeds and de- nominational doctrines to all children alike, which in the case of School Boards could not be done.

We have religious education in our Board Schools that is quite sufficient for many ft ienda of education, who are also ministers of the Gospel; and why ratepayers generally should be asked to support any sect to which they do not belong it is difficult to understand. If the ratepayers in Earlestown have their eyes open they will ignore the circular just addressed to them, with such an unparelled amount of impudence. If a rate is to be paid at all, it must be paid to a representative and responsible body, which will account to the ratepayers for all monies received and paid away. In this manner alone will it be possible to have a fair administra- tion of the Elementary Education Act of 1891, in- stead of the one-sided management and extraordin- ary tactics we have lately had the pleasure of witnessing. If the school authorities think that any provisional arrangement in the shape of a voluntary rate is likely to be approved of by the ratepayers, they will soon find out the enormity of their delusion. The election campaign at Accringtob, which ended so satisfactorily to the Liberal party. brought out some of the worst features of the recognised. Labour leaders. Hitherto they have been credited. with a modicum of common sense, but their action with regard to the contest at Accrington plainly stamps them as injudicious advisers of the people. They were evidently not born to become leaders of the political fortunes of our working classes. The arrant behaviour of men like Mr. ILeir Hardie, M.P., and Mr. Tom Mann brings ignominy upon the labour cause, and excites the disgust or a moderate section of the labour army. It Keir Hardie wants to ventilate his particular fads either in the house of Commons or outside, let him do soindependently and without expectation of any support from intel- ligent and fair minded Liberals. Something differ- ent was certainly expected of Tom Mann, but per- haps all the talk about him entering the Church in the daily press has led him to over estimate his own

muortance. Honest John Burns is a man of a far different type to either of the afore mentioned couple, and can see a great deal further. He condemned forci- bly the advice given to the workingmen of Accring- ton to abstain from voting, and perci iVed the folly of adopting such a course. What the Independent Labour party could hope to gain by letting a Tory obtain possession i of a Liberal seat it is impossible to see. Tory action n the past stamps them RS being antagonistic to the needs of working men; and it is hardly conceivable that they will concede their privileges, and ideas of social position, in order to extend the powers and improve the condition of our democratic workers. The sacrifice they would be called upon to make would be too great; and Tories, of all people, like to feel hew important it is that they iihould I3e the gover- nors and directors. of the British nation. Their fathers (ancestors is .the word they like) ruled the people, and they think they should do the same. Their tide has gone by and they know it.

The man to be pitied most, just now is poor Her- mon Hodge, the defeated Tory at Accrington. His wobblings over the " Contracting out " clause have gained for him an unenvitable notority, and Em- lovers' Liability will cease to have any charms for him, at least for a time. The telegram to the House lof Commons op the night when Earl Dudley's amendment was being considered, caused great amusement to those who had read his reply to the deputation that waited upon him. with respect to the clause. It would have been better for him bad he allowed the matter to rest at the conclusion of the contest. Apparently Mr. Hedge thinks other- wise. In a letter to the " Times " he gives additional evidence in favoi,r of the attitude be took on the matter, which, if he had made himself clearly un- derstood at the beginning, ought not now to be required. Anyhow he is now regarded as a" wob- bier," and it will be some time before the electors, can forget how he came to earn the name. Mr. Hermon Hodge, like many other Tory candidates, finds it extremely difficult to please Tory electors and the Tory officialdom at the same time, and es- pecially in the middle of an election.

The readers of the "Christian World "have a treat in store for the New Year, and one which is to be appreciated. With the issue of that journal on January 4th, will appear the first instalment of a new novel written by that popular novelist ". Edna Lyall." It is ,•egarded asia notable work and is en- titled " Doreen : The Story of a Singer." It is mentioned as a story of Irish rights and wrongs,and Mr. Gladstone's attention having been called to this fact by a member of Parliament, be has written,ex- pressing his great interest and gratification for this information. The public too will join in the warm • appreciation of the works of the popular novelist, which the Premier generously refers to ; and all who have read "Donovan," " We Two," and " Knight Errant," will anxiously await the ap- pearance of " Doreen." The proprietors of the "Christian World "ought to be rewarded or their enterprise by a large increilse in the c r.:u ation of their, piptr, which is in this case well deserved. G.P.O.

Ureic the whole we are disposed to • A Duko's think bllalo the Duke OI.C4DINBURGEI £10,000.. would have done better if he had, upon becoming Duke of Saxe-Coburg and Gotha, , renounced the £lO,OOO a Year which, amongst ' other substantial sums, he has teen. iu the habit of receiving from the English taxpayers. Had he done so he would hays6stood higher in the estima- tion of Englishmen, and it can hardly be doubted that sitch an act of renunciation would have strengthened his position in the small German Principality to the throne of which be has suc- ceeded. Tne Duke has acquired the reputation, deservedly or undeservedly, of being "close- fisted "—an unpopular quality in a prince—Land his latest act will not tend to obliterate that im- pression. Besides, the ruler of an independent State does not add to,his dignity by becoming the pensioner of another kingdom. Sovereigns who in the past have so far humiliated themselves as to accept the gratuities of foreign coun- tries have, as a rule, been held up to derision and condemnation in histery. It is true that in this case the Duke is the son of the English Queen. As such, and as a subject of.the Rritish throne and a naval officer, he has shared in the profuse liberality with which Parliament treats the Royal family. HA? might have continued to enjoy that liberality till the end of his dap had

he chosen to remain a British subject, and te retain his connection with the Royal navy. Ile was under no imperative obligations to accept the Dukedom of Saxe-Coburg, and, indeed, it is said that the Germans have been not a little dis- appointed that he did not stand aside in favour of his eldest son who has been trained in GermanY• But he elected to step in as the successor Of his uncle, and having, by that free choice renounced his allegiance to Great Britian, and accepted the position of an independent sovereign,, it might have been expected that he would at the same time have given up all the emoluments whicb'. appertained to the place he has resigned. The DUKE has, however, not done this, and it fell to the lot of Mr. GLAnsTotqa to announce the decision of the DIME to Parliament and the country. may be easily believed that the veteran Premier' would have been gladly excused from such a task. It has been said in palliation of the Max's dee' , sion that the income of his little kingdom is on e very limited scale, and that his private inh ritance ' from his' predecessor takes, for the most party the

font of debts. Those who write or speak in this vein ignere, of course, the fact that the Dilke exercised a perfectly free choic3 in ceepting new honours. He had amp's opportu ties 0! baian'cing the respective tv:vtua. tagee or sadvsn"

tages of the two Courses that. wer,-: pen to

The. one thing that he was not entitled • recl upon was that, ,whilst accepting a position , re state and independence, he should also be alio'

to retain a large part of the pecuniary benefit that accrued to him through his being a ruerabe of the reigning family of England and a Britisl subject. But, apart from that, this plea ° poverty—for that is what it really amounts tO very Strongly contradicted. Official figures 811°1

. _ that his income as Duke of Saxe-Cobnrg

Gotha will be over £30,000 a year, while it is

that the Duchess receives from her brotterf tl Czar of Russia, at least an equal amount. In a dition to this it is said that the Crown possessiet of Saxe-Coburg are very considerable and yield handsome revenue; and then the Dake—a "

careful man, it is understood, in regard to mone7 matters—will have his "private investments. A' together,. it seems very clear that the nelf sovereign of Saxe-Coburg:might, without reduou3g himself to straitened; circumstances, have fore- gone the British pension of £10,004.). That Ore would be much more usefully spent in this oolle(i try in relieving ,the distress of the •unemploY? than in maintaining the ridiculous and foie pageantry of ri" small German court.

' COUNT TOLSTOI, the fanaolB Rua- • As others see na. sian writer, has been interviewed lately, and has spoken with his usual originality and directness on several sub- . jects. He admits that Englishmen possess many noble qualities, but—and it is always the 'way with thesofforeigners—he will have it that there is a " terrible prevalence of hypocrisy and cant " in this country. He obstinately refuses, for instance, to see tho religion and philanthropic side of the doings of the Chartered Company of South Africa. Ile says, "How men can reconcile conquest, mur- der, pillage and robbery with the Church of Christ awnixot understand.' Those who act thus may be Christians of the Churches, but they are crimina. ls none the less. There are," he added, two kinds of criminals; those who break the laws and those who act as criminals, under the Protection of the law. The Chartered Company's servants belong to the category of the latter." Language of this kind must be. really painful reading to that chaplain of the Company's forces whose letters, it will be remembered, were such a Strange mixture of militancy and piety. Then, the Count is rather heterodox, too, with regard to •the suhject of a "living wage" for workmen. Ho 18 struck, like many Others, with the indefiniteness of the term. In his own case,-he isable to live on al3bout a penny a day since ho became a vegetarian, formerly his daily food cost about six shillings. Übe many others, however, he thinks that the (`:ll3' effectual settlement of disputes in the coal 'rade will be found in the nationalisation of the zaines.

___ " THE MESSIAH" AT ReNcsitx. A very Wee and appreciative audience assembled in Runcorn Public Hall on Christmas night, on the occasion of the performance of Handel's " Messiah " by the Runcorn Tonic' Sol-fa Prize Choir. This is the third successive year that the choir have performed this masterpiece, of oratorio during the appropriate season of Chrisityastide, and that it fills up a blank is abundantly testified by the patronage bes- towod. Mr.Holford, and the choir are to be heartily con- , gratulated upon their performance of Monday night. I The familiar choruses were sung with the exactness for ' which the choir is noted, but also with a feeling and ' power that left little to be desired, and the audience ex- pressed their approval in an unmistakable tbanner. The principals were MiSS Moorhouse, Miss Fish, Mr. G. H. Lawton and Mr. Eaten Batty, and they proved an effi- cient quartet. Miss Nlnorhouse has a soprano voice of great range, and she sang with much taste and expres- sion, a remark which also applies to the • voealisatietrof Miss fish. ' Mr. Lawton has a plessinz voice which he usea to the best advantage, and 'Mr. Batty, Who is DO stranger to Runcorn, sang with his accustomed effec- tiveness. TOE TOWN HALL EXTENSION.

Mr. LONGTON asked whether, in view of the contem- plated extension of the Warrington Town Hall, it had been considered that the Board was in need of more cen- tral accommodation. If there was going to be any altfration, it might come before the Board for considera- tioe,—The CLERK replied that the subject of having central offices in Warrington for Union purposes was one that had been before the Board upon many occasions in years gone by. If a central building could be obtained, the Board room could then be used for Workhouse pur- pcnes. As the population increased,Ehey would no doubt sconer or later require additional buildings for the insti- tution, and particularly there was the question of the infirmary. He thought that it would be both a useful and a prudent thing to do if they could have combined effices for rural as well as for urban purpoies in a central position. Ile was afraid, however, that they could not follow out the su4gesflori of Mr. Longton. It w.s always the rule to have the Union offices separate from the municipal offices. Of course, they could be near the Town Hall. If Mr. Longton could see his way to move the appointment of a small committee of expe- rienced Guardians in order to c.fisider the subject it would be useful in fixing upon something—Mr. . LONO- TON said that he would consider the matter further,and bring the subject before the next Board ineetiog:—After further remarks by Mr. Heaton the subject dropped.

TTTE EX AM IN-E": SATUVD AY. DECEMBER 30 1893.

PROPOSED NEW EYF-LAWS FOR RUNCORN. ' TO THR EDITOR OF THE " ERLMINBIt." • Sir, —We see that you have been kind enough to give a rull report in your valuable paper of meetings of the Improvement Commissionere of Ituncoru as to the new. bye-laws, and we know you are always willing to give Justice to both sides of a question so very important to the town and the welfare of from Ii property owners and tenants. &mat of the Commissiohers Have felt the responsibility of adopting bye-laws which they may not have thoroughly understood themselves in all their de- tails, and therefore have been kind enough to ask a few ratepayers and contractors of the town whom they thought had experience in such matters from their daily occupations in building houses, &c., in differeut towns as to their opinion on several clauses. After con- sultation there were a few gentlemen found willing to go through the bye-laws and is meeting was called tor the 10:11 November. At that meeting it was proposed that the secretary pro.tens. should ask the secretary to the Commissioners for two copies of their intended bye-laws. His reply was that We could only have ticopy by paying sixpence per hundred words of the same, whicn was naturally declined, especially when the ratepayers were already paying for the printing, and while the type was set Would net cast more than say 63. a copy extra. but we afterwards foundthree of the Commiesanters willing to lend us theirs. At our next meeting, November 13tii, we got through clauses from 7 CO 50. amid met again en the 14th November When the further clauses were gone through. It was then decided for our secretary to write to the chairman of the Commissioners, as the time was limited by their notice, of our objections to certain clauses—the, particulars of which we give afterwards— and some questions that required answering as to what water closet they intended to adept, there being nothing definite in the bee-laws. Therefore there was no law as to whose water closet a contractor or ratepayer should use. We also wished to ask questions as to tee aslipits, as the bee-laws give one of 40 cubic feet capacity or three months, one of eight cubic feet and another of six cubic feet or seven days. We merely wanted information upon, the above, and at the same time told the chairman that , we should be willing to meet a committee (if the Runcorn! Commissioners by appointment to go through each clause and give our te asous for objections. The secretary wrote saying he nad received our com- munication, and it would receive attention, date.d 16dt Nov. We afterwards received (Nov. 28th) a letter saying that the committee would be prepared to receive the de- putation as requested on the 6th Dec., at half past ten a.m. The deputation met the Committee at the public 10fileee as per eote, and they were then toed by the chair- man that the committee would not go through any clause of the bye laws, nor answer any question ter they were passed and adopted by them. Two members then got up arid said they were not passed. Their alter they had . crossed swords-before us and agreed between themselves thee they did not know what they had passed, which quite pleased us, we retired. Now we cannot under- stand what made them invite us to meet them at all if they did not intend to anisver our questions—was it merely to insult us ? For we did not ask to meet them, but we should be willing—that is if they requested us. The following are some of the points which we raised objection to aud required answering : BYE LAWS. Page 7—Clause 6, to force every one to put a layer of concrete 6in. thick on the"vehole of the ground serfaee Of site of a building. Where there is good solid rock like some parts of Runcorn that you build upon, why go to the expense of cutting away good rock arid then put a 6in. layer of cement concrete, when one inch layer of pure cement . would make a better job and seal elm pores of the rock up, concrete being no better than stone to start with ? Page 11—Letter part of clause 13, as to damp course over every double or cavity wall near the roof we con- sider useless. `• We agree with the one in walls, on the ground level." We also consider the 2iin. cavity walls useless, as they stand in the bye laws. The rain or other moisture striking the face ot an outside wall will go through to the cavity. The moisture in the cavity will strike through the inside wall, affecting the plaster and after- wards rotting the raper and paste attached to walls in rooms Rod forming a poisoned atmosphere and unfit for human hetita tint's., We propose or suggest that there should be ventilating grids Oin. by 3in. in the top and bottom of steel cavity walls, famine a through Ali' cer- rent and so allow all damp air to escape or be forced out. This is not in the bs e laws, but still it is done in several buTlings in Runcorn and we consider it a very import- ant matter. It does seem strange that the great experts did not see it, but we feel sure the Medical Officer will see the bepefit of it, especially where there is so muce green poisonous papers used on walls.
