TH6 PIioCESSION UP THE 8111? CANAL LOCAL ARRANGEMENTS.

The procession ot vessels which is to pass along the chip Canal on New Year's Day (Monday), will start from near the Latchford Locks. The time fixed for the commencement of the procession is AM. It will be of a miscellansous character and will consist of sailing and steam vessels, cargo and pleasure boats. Dining the week several vessels have passed along the waterway to Latchford to be ready to join in the procession. • The line of craft will be headed by the Norseman, a yacht which will contain the chief officials of the Ship Canal Com- pany, as well aS a number of the directors. Follow- ing this vessel will come the "Snowdrop," the first , boat which plied on the Canal, and she will contain ha members of the Manchester City Council. When Mode Wheel is reached the order of procedure will he ckanged. The "Snowdrop" will enter the locks first, and such ot the Corporation directors as are on board the Norseman will join the Lord Mayor and their colleagues of the City Council. hi this Way, as is quite fitting, the boat of the Lord Mayor, aldermen and councillors of Manchester„—beariug also the members of the Art Gallery and Technical Instruction Committees who have not seats in the City Council—will be the first to steam into the docks at Pomona. The time fixed for the arrival of the Snowdrop and the Norsemen at Manchester is 110011. . . .

Several local boats will join in the procession,and the members of the Warrington Corporation will also make the voyage along the whole of the canal. They will leave Latchford at 9.30 in the Helvetia, kindly lent by Messrs. Monks, Hall and Company. The vessel has been specially fitted up for the-trip, and suitable accommodation will be provided. The party expect to reach Manchester Docks at 3.30, but this will greatly depend upon the time spent in going through the various locks. ! The task of passing through these 'places may be considerably prolonged on account of the large number of vessels which will have to use the locks. It is not expected that the Mayor (Alderman IC. Greenall), will be present, though most of the members of the Council and the chief officials rA the Corporation have accepted invitations. A number of pleasure boats will run from Liverpool during the morning. The Warrington Corporation also chartered a couple of barges to bring up cargoes of setts from Saltport, but as it is not possible to get these crafts towed along the canal, they will not make the trip uutil later in the week. Messrs. Crostleld and Sons intend to run their three steam boass Gertrude, Ada, and George. They will join in the procession at Latchford. Each vessel -will contain a cargo of soap, and there will be on board a party of the firm's customers. Fol- lowing these vessels will come Messrs! Fairclough's two steam vessels, Pixie and Pioneer. They will be freighted with cargoes of flour, and will have on board as many invited guests as can be accommo- dated. Last week we mentioned the fact that the Warrington Slate Company bad chartered a vessel to bring slates horn Port Dinorwic to Manchester. It was arranged at first that the vessel shouldloin in the procession at Latchford ; but the company have been notified that the cargo cannot be shipped in time to allow the vessel to reach thecanal before the middle of the week.

- It is expected that altogether there will he 70 sea-going vessels Which will simply pass along the canalanL upon reaching Salford and Illanchester Docks, be berthed at the quays R 9 expeditiously as possible. The directors deem i necessary that the public should be warned as to the danger of approaching too near to the edge of the canal or wharv-m. At the same time they feel that great interest will be taken by the shareholders and the public in Os first arrival of vessels in lilancliester and Salford,-and they bare arranged to issue passes to all shareholders who may apply lor them, admit- ting to the Trafford wharf on the Stretford side of the canal, or to the Salford quay, adjoining the racecourse. No other persons will be admitted to the docks or above Barton except on business, but the banks of the canal between Barton Bridge and Warrington will he available for the general public, excepting at the locks. The shareholders and the public generally are earnestly requested to assist the company by keeping clear ot the leeks and quays, particularly when resjels are being hert'o.ed, discharged,or loaded. The risk of being accept into UAICII dos.k.,..L.At many points very :,•rettr.qtvl it would be—almost iinpossittle to provideAitti_tient appliances at all points .where danger exists. The directors feel sure that the shareholders and the public will co-operace with them as far as possible in this matter.

AN INTERVIEW WITH MR. LEADER WILLIAMS. Fqw pmlishmen have any idea of the magnitude of this undertaking (says an interviewer in " To Day "), either because they compare it ti) an ordinary waterway or because they imagine that nothing can surpass the Suez Canal.. In reality, however, the grandeur of the works on the Manchester Snip Canal is far more impo.s- ire:. 'De Leaseps had 'very few engineering difficulties to overcome. Ilia canal joins two seas, it traverses a sandy and desert plain, it is built on a level, and all that was required to keep its banks sip was to give them a gentle slope. The Suez Canal had no vested interests'on either sida ; it had no railway lines, much less other canals (which could tint be diverted from their course), crossing it. /it light angles. Finally, it is much narrower thee its sister canal in Cottononnlis. •• Yes. our work—which I am glad to say is nearinr.! eompletion—was incomparably more difficult than Suez." TheFe were the _words in which my interlocetor, Mr. E. LeaderWfiliams, chief engineer of the Manchester Ship Canal, rave expression to his opinion. "Yes. Our difficulties began before von a plan was adopted, for we Mgt to contend with and remove the powerful opposition against the scheme in Parliament— opposition which wp. opported by the Corporation and the Deck Board of Liverpool, and some of the largest railway companies." " 150 you think that the canal will in any way be de- trimental to the prosperity of Liverpool ?" " Certa'nly to some extent ; but as trade increases considerably every year, in a short time there will be work for both of us. But there le no doubt that at the commencement Liverpool will feel the loss': You must bear in mind that Mancheeer anti the country within a radios of twenty miles is the busiest and most densely- populated district in the world." "Were there any other plane before yours?" "Oh, yes ; the idea of this canal is old, the late Mr. Fulton was in favour of bringing the sea to Manchester direct without hocks; but the difficulties inherent to this plan were so great that it had to be abandoned. Several otier p:ojects were prcseuted before wine was

approved and accepted. My original idea was to con- struct the canal from Liverpool to Runcorn by dredging in the centre of the tidal estuary of the Mersey, which is nearly dry at Iw tide, a canal deep enough to enable ships of any size to sail up to Runcorn at all times. But this gave rise to a most heated controversy. The opponents of the canal, in their desire to wreck the whole scheme, objected that the works on the estuary would Cause accretions such as had occurred on the Dee and other rivers. I finally had to give up this plan for the present more costly one, by which instead of enter- ing the Mersey at Runcorn, the canal is continued down to Eastham, six miles above Liverpool. This portion of the canal is, nearly the whole way, constructed on the river bed, and is separated from the Mersey only by a bank." Mr. Williama then suggested that I should visit the ;Manchester and Salford Docks,the terminus of the canal. I was joined there by Mr. James Deas, jun., engineer of the docks anel of the Salford section of the canal, and by Mr. F. L. Williams, son of the chief engineer, and resi- dent engiheer of the dry dock, built alongside the canal. I really only formed a true estlmate of the magnitude of the works when I got fairly on the ground, and I did not hesitate to give expression to my admiration. • My interlocutors satisfied my curiosity by kindly vo• lunteeri rig to give the following description of the works: ' —"The canal is 35i miles long, but although not so long as Suez, it was in many respects a far more formidable engineering task. De Lesseps gz.,t the land free of cost, and almost unlimited native labour at very low rates of pay. All he had to do was to remove the sand and make the banks with flat slopes. The Manchester Canal is a river canal ; its water's level is 80ft. higher in Manches- ter than at Eastham. In order to obtain this rise the canal has foar sets of double locks, which divide it in five sections : the first section frotn Eastbarn to Latch- ford Locks, is 21 miles long ; the second.to Irlate Leeks 7i miles ; the third to Barton Locks, 2 miles ; the fourth to Mole Locks, 3f miles long ; the fifth section, which contains the vast Salford docks, is a mile and three quarters in length. The double locks, dividing one sec- tion from the other, are 800 ft. and 3501 t. long respec- tively. The minimum width at the bottom of the canal from Eastham tip Barton Locks is 120 ft., and at the water level 175 ft. From Barton to Manchester these widths increase to 170 ft. and 250 ft. respectively ; thus, with very few exceptions, the largest ship afloat can get to Manchester. Near the teriminus this width increases largely, in order to enable steamers to move and turn round easily without interfering with the ordinary navigation." In reply to m 5 question relative to the prospects ol the canal, Mr. Williams expressed the conviction th ultimately it ihl be a great success. " Slat prevents Manchester becoming almost the largest city in Enelatail are the freights from and to Liverpool. High class goods pay 13s. 81. per ton at present. Ry Canal the freight fixed by an Act of Par- liament will not exceed 7s. %With this reduced freight, Manchester in a few years will double its activity. The shipping interests are folly alive to this and three lines of steamers are alrt aly wive' tised to leave Manchester on January 2 for Belfast, London, and Glasgow. There is at the present moment, moreover, a large number of vessels loading in different U.S. harbours for ManAirs- ter."

Mr. E. Leader Williams is persevering, affable, and lively, and had it not been for him it i 3 doubtful if the canal would ever have been constructed. He belong.s to a family of engineers, his father having directed the works of the !liver Severn navigation.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN,

AT IVA It RINUTON.

Mr.H.J.Longton referred, at the fortnightly meet- - ing of the Bsuird of Guardians on Wednesday,to the proposed extension of the Town hail, and wished to know whether the Guardians could not have some share of the extra accommodation, The Guardians have not a very imposing structure in which to transact their business at present, and are greatly in want, of a more ceirral building. The Clerk,. however, while agreeing that the present premises might be utilized for the inmates, if the Guardians took another building, did not care for the union buildings to be connected with the municipal build- ings. tie would not object to them being near each other. Of course there is a factor to think of in any scheme of this kind. The rates are at present very - high, and it would be well to pause before expend- ing money before it becomes absolutely necessary. The Guardians did not come to any conclusion on the matter on Wednesday, but there will probably be more said about the subject at their neat meet- ing, when Mr. Longton has promised to. bring it up again.

The Guardians had the subject of the superan- nuation of their medical officer, Dr. Spinks, before them for discussion on Wednesday. One or two of them, notably Mr. R. Heaton and Mr, J. Niuir, were apparently very ewers: to the discussion being pub- lic, but they were almost by themselves, so that the Guardians did not "go into committee," With respect to the subject before them they were in a , peculiar position. Dr. Spinks is a permanent offi- cer of the Union, and, having completed 40 years' service, is thereby entitled to a maximum superan- nuation of two-thirds of his salary upon retiring. Tne majority of the Guardians were desirous of having a new man at the post, but they could not c-Ompel Dr. Spigks to resign as he had not been guilty of neglect of duty. Dr. Spinks himself offered to resign provided that he were granted the maximum superannuation. A motion calling upon the doctor to resign and accepting his conditions was carried unanimously, the merobern perceiving that, there was no other choice left to them. It is to be hoped that in appointing a new medical offi- cer, the Guardians will adopt the advice that was given them, and have a privat agreement tor a six months' notice. This would prevent all unpleasant- ness in future.

• ---- The statements made bt the Borough Court on, Tuesday by Mr. W. Pierpoint, and Mr. hunt (chief constable) point to the fact that in Warrington at least there has been a considerable amount of drinking during the festive season, notwithstand- ing the recent distre,s in the towns, and the si ruitened circum3tances in which many p, opre wete placed. DESERTER.—Edward Handy was remanded until Tues- day, on a charge of being a deserter, from Her Majesty's Royal Marines stationed in Coventry, Bleu February 10, 1890. AN IMPUDENT THEFT FROM A DWELLING•HOUSE. Thomas Lowe was charged with stealing frcin a dwell- ing-house of Seth Turner, 59 Folly-lane, on Sunday,three men's vests, two pairs at trousers, a pair of stockings, and two coats, valued 40s. Mr. Hunt said that the prisoner was a furnaceman, and had worked for three weeks at Dallam forge. He was a native of Liverpool. On Sunday afternoon,Mr. and Mrs. Turner went to visit a friend in Winwick-road, leaving their daughter Iletty at home. The front door was locked but the back door was unlocked. The daughter was considerably surprisedi some time after her parents had gone away, by seeing a I man look out of the back door, and then go into the I house again. She immediately ran after her parents, and her father and a man named Crank ran back. Crank was the first to arrive, and he ran in by the back way, and saw the prisoner getting out by the front. Be ran through the house, and on his way stumbled against some clothing upon the floor, which the prisoner had evidently been getting together ready to take away with him. Crank followeci the prisoner into Allcard-st., and there caught him and took him back to the house,where it was discovered that lie had three waistcoats upon him which belonged to the complainant. The boxes upstairs were ransacked from top to bottom. Prisoner had taken off his boots so as to make no noise, and in his haste to get away,he had left them behind. He was subsequently , handed over to the police.—Prisoner said that tie was drunk or be would not have done such a thing.—He was sent to gaol for a month. DRUNK IN CHARGH ON A HORSE AND CART.—Patrick McCormick, cab driver, was tincd ss. and costs for be- ing drunk in charge of a horse and cart in Wiuwick-st., on Dec. 23rd. P.C. Loxham proved the case. Defendant was fined ss. and costs or seven days. THE BA.TTLESHIP IN A GALE.

AN OFFICERS THRILLING STORY.

Admiral Algernon De Horsey writes tq the "Times.": —You may perhaps consider the following letter of sufficient interest for publication. The writer is un- known to me,but a distinguished officer, who forwarded to me the letter, states that he is a good officer with plenty of pluck, and scientific in a practical way. The resolution may or may not be of good design for fighting, and it is possible (although I do not admit it) that the dire exigencies of war necessitate top-heavy and west aworthy ships. However this may be, nothing can excuse a new iron ship being eo constructively weak as to become dangerously strained and leaky from the effects of one gale. As to the probable result if in the height of the gale the engines or steering gear had broken down, and the ship bad fallen off in the trough of the sea, I hesitate to express an opinion; but I do trust that the authorities will cease to put a blind confidence in theoretical calculations ot stability. Resolution, at Sea, Dec. 22, 1893.

My dear—,—ln all my experience as a naval man I have never gone through such a terrifying time as I have during the last few days. • We left Plymouth last Monday for Gibraltar. In all this class of ship the stability question has, of course, played a great part. All tlse other vessels of Allis class were tested for stability before they left England, but in our case the stability is a matter of theory, based upon calculations made at the Constructor's department, the result being that our maximum angle of stability is 38deg., with a rapidly declining chance of righting, until a vanishing point of 65 deg. is reached, when all is over. Fancy the pleasant sensation of finding her rolling in moderate weather 42 deg. each way directly she got into the bay. This did not occur only once. Our confidence in the ship having received such a rude shock, we on Wednesday last experienc<el a gale the equal of which I have never seen. Between the squalls the sea rolled mountains high. Our forebridge is thirty-seven feet from the water and some distance from the bows. From that the approaching wall of water blocked out any view of the horizon. Forty feet is a low estimate of the height of these rollers. During the squalls the whole surface of the sea was torn up and simply driven along in a sheet of spray mixed with hailstones. The sight and noise were horrifying. All this time we steamed slowly, keeping her head to the sea, but knowing full well that any deviation of the held), any breakdown of tha engines or steam-steering gear and we should broach to and capsize. ' During the night of the gale the water came below in tons, and at one time the engine-room had five feet of ' water in it. This ran a chance of putting the fires out. Everything was then closed up except one small h atch- way amidships, well protected, and un and down this for eighteen hours everyone of the 700 pe•-ple in the ship had to go. The atmosphere below became filthy and poisonous. The next day, when the gale slightly moderated and coal was running short, it became a question of what shall we do ? but ,we dare not turn round for fear of her going over. At length at 4 a.m., yesterday (Thursday) the sea had gone down sufficiently to allow of our altering our course a couple of points and making for Queenstown, which we hope to

-reach

this evening. _ I had no fault to find with the gale or with the ship during the gale. She performed her part splendidly so far as it went. What is certainly a serious matter is this evident want of stability. There is not a man of mature age in the ship but feels he is in a thoroughly unsafe ship. . . . . We ought not to leave England without being tested, and there 3ught most assuredly to be a redistri- bution of weights..--Ever yours.

The two defendants were arraigned in court a month ago, but the case was adjourned in order to give them another chance. In- spector uphill said he had visited the house three or four times since the last hearing of the case. The con- dition of the children was better, and they were better clothed, but the condition of the house and surroundings were in a filthy dirty condition, and the bedding was insufficient. He asked the magistrates to adjourn the case for another month. The application was granted, the chairman remarking that the magistrates were quite ' determined to have the condition of the house to the satisfaction of the officer, as there was no excuse for dirt. Threw OF • GrAss.—Ellen Carless, of 24, Ship Yard, was charged with stealing from the Legh Arms at a quarter to seven that morning, a glass, value 3d., the property of Jas. Henry James, the landlord. The evidence showed that the prisoner accompanied by several others went into the Legh Arms Inn, Mersey-st., about six o'clock that morning, but the barmaid refused to serve her with drink. Soon after leaving the premises the prisoner was met by P.C. Clough, and the glass prcduced was found under her shawl. Edward White, barman. identified the glass as belonging to the landlord of the Legh Arms. The prisoner was com- mitted to gaol for fourteen days. -- • • •

Mr. Shearer, relieving efficer, said that the prisoner was allowed to leave the Workhouse for the purpose of attending chapel. She should have come back in time for dinner, but instead of that she returned about half-past two, drunk. She was not fit to mix with the other inmates, and was put in a room by herself. She got out and became very abusive, threw things at several of the officers, and eventually had to be put in the receiving ward. There she broke two panes of glass and was sent down to the police station.—The porter, ' James Curry, bore out the above statement, and denied the allegations of the prisoner that undue violence had been used by him. Mr. Garnett (the chairman) asked the Chief Constable whether he had ascertained where the woman had obtained the drink. The prisoner said a friend had given her some in tea. Mr. Pierpoint said on Saturday night the town wad a perfect pandemonium. The Chief Constable said a great deal of drinking had been done in the homes. He didn't remember see- ing so many bottles in use. The prisoner was dismissed with a caution. -

RUGBY.

WARRINGTON V. LANCASTER. - These teams met at Lancaster on Saturday. Laacaster had made a special whip for the accasion, their team including the brothers Storey, of University fame. John Bate was the only notable absentee on the Warrington side. The recent reins had rendered the ground heavy, especially on the reserved side of the touch. It was twenty minutes late when Lancaster kicked against the slight breeze. The visitors had the best deb() opening exchanges, but capi- tal forward play by Lancaster caused Heesom some anxiety. The latter eared well, but Lancaster kept up the pressure, and as a result of capital passing Thomp- son scored, Storey failing at the place. CHARGE OF HIGHWAY ROBBERY.

PRISONERS COMMITTED TO THE ASSIZES. John Shields and Thomas Garrity, two well- known characters to the police, were charged at the Borough Court on Tuesday morning with as- saulting and robbing Charles W. Smith, in. Bank- at., at a quarter to nine on Saturday night, of a purse containing 4s. 7d.—The Chief Constable (Mr. Hutit) said that the complainant was a labourer on the Ship Canal, and lodged at 48, Red-row, Wash-lane,Latch- ford. On Saturday night complainant met Shields on Warrington Bridge, Shields accosted him,and asked him to "stand a drink," as he was on tramp. Smith con• seated, and they went to the lOse and Crown public- house. After drinking there they went to the Shakespeare Dolman's-lane. After having a small glass of beer there he left with the intention of going for a shave. He proceeded along Dolman's-lano, down Higher Bank-st., and when opposite Ship-yard, Shields came up unawares and knocked him down. He thrust his hand in com- plainant's pocket and drew out his purse. Garrity came up, and complainant felt himself kicked about the head by somebody. He shouted out t' He is stealing my purse." A soldier named Quine heard what was taking place, and he wrested the purse from Shields. While he was doing this he was kicked by Garrity. Shields got away, but Garrity was brought to the Police Station. Shields was apprehended later on in the Bridge Inn by Constables Lewis and Bruen. He fought on the way to the Police Station, and it took three officers to take him there.—Charles W.Smith,the camplainant,gave evidence in corroboration of the Chief Constable's statement.— Samuel Quinn, a private of the Liverpool Regiment, sta- tioned at Orford Barracks, said he was in the house of Mrs. Madden, Bank-st.,when hearing a disturbance out- side, he ran out and saw Shields struggling with the complainant. Shields had him by the throat against the wall, taking his purse off him. In the struggle they both fell to the ground, Shields being uppermost. Be saw Shields take the purse out of complainant's pocket and run away with it. He (witness) ran after Shields and knocked him down, and took the purse from him. sidlile he was doing this Garrity kicked him.—PS. Cheney deposed that while in company with Constable Sankey on Saturday night, he received Garrity into cus- tody from Quine. Quine said. that Garrity had helped to rob a man. He was brought to the Police Station, and when charged he said : " I did not touch the man." Shields was charged later on with robbing complainant, and replied, "It's offside.'!—Constable Lewis proved ar- resting Shields.—Shields, in reply to the magistrates, did not deny the charge, but stated that Garrity was not with him. Garrity also denied taking any part in the affair, and called a witness in support of his statement. —Both prisoners were committed to the Liverpool Assizes.

MR. LABOUCHERE AND MR. GLAD.

===== 0002601_18931230_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
STONE.

Writing in "Truth" on Wednesday, Mr. Labouchere I says :—Last summer Mr. Gladstene's exertions were prodigious. For months he held the field night after 1 night, in a discussion which tried the staying powers of • younger men, but which apparently produced no ill- ' effect upon him. But the laws of nature are unalter- able, no force can be exerted witnout a waste of force. Ia youth, and indea, up to the confines at old age, abnormal exertion has no ill results, for it is met by recuperation. In extreme old age it tells. No one can watch Mr. Gladstone in the House of Commons without perceiving that the work of the summer has greatly aged him. Now, although Ido not always agree with him in every-thing, there is no man for whom I have a greater admiration, for I believe that no man has so truly devoted himself during a long life to his country's interests. I do, therefore, hope that his entourage will, insist upon his passing the two most trying months of the winter—January and February—in a mild climate, as has been his wont in past years. That the House of Commons is apt to get out of hand in the absence of its leader is true ; for, whatever the ability and tact of his toe= t6te7is may be, he is without the authority that is involved in actual leadership. Bub this consideration ought not to weigh in the balance against Mr. Gladstone's health. The Tories though they attack him, are proud of him as a living evidence of the staying qualities of the Anglo- Saxon race, and they would be the last to wish that he should not continue to surprise the world by his peren- nial vigour for many a year, although possibly they might prefer that he should become the Patriarch of Hawarden rather than the Athlete of Westminster. When Voltaire's admirers insisted upon his coming to Paris, and taking part there in theatrical performances and meetings of the Institute, we know what were the results. Yet Voltaire did not du in a week during his stay in Paris what Mr. Gladstone often does in a day. It is all nonsense to suppose that a man of eighty-live can incur the responsibilities and worries involved in the rule of an Empire, and the work of depending his policy in an assembly where his opponents are nearly as num- erous as his supporters, without serious risk, and I con- fess that I do not like to see Mr. Gladstone exposed to this risk. He incurs it from a sense of duty. lam by no means certain that the country has a right to demand that the risk should be continued, and I for one should— politics apart—rejoice were he to follow the example of other veterans who, when younger than hev is, withdrew ' from the strife and turmoil of party to a well-earned repose. At least, however, if he does insist on devoting himself to his country's service, we ought to see that the burden lies as lightly upon him as possible.

RUNCORN RURAL SANITARY

--AUTHORITY.

The fortnightly meeting was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. Mr. Mclntyre presided and there were present the Rev. J. W. N. Tanner, Messrs. Whitley, Hey woed, Aaron, Woodward, Ford, Pickering, Knowles, Lewis, Williams, Brandreth. J. Ashton (clerk) G. Ashton (deputy clerk) J. Farrington (sanitary inspector) and Dr. Adams (medical officer). COMPLIMENTS OF THE SEASON. The Chairman expressed the hope that all had spent a happy Christmas and the wish that they would base a prosperous New. Year. (Hear, hear). MOORE SMALLPDX HOSPITAL. A letter was read from the Manchester Ship Canal Company stating that they had a smallpox hospita situated on a spoil bank near Moere. for which they had no use; would the Runcorn Sanitary authority care to purchase 2—The Clerk was instructed to inquire the terms upon which the hospital could be acquired. WESTON WATER SUPPLY. Mr. Aaron inquired whether it was not a fact that before a house could be occupied in the Authority's dis- trict, the Inspector must see that the house was properly drained,and supplied with water,and that everything else was in a sanitary condition.—The Clerk replied in the affirmative.—Mr. Aaron asked what was to be done in cases where the houses were inhabited and there was no water supply. In Weston there were many houses without any supply of water. In his own house he had -a tap in the cellar, and he had been unable to get water there. Could not the Authority take some action against the Runcorn Commissioners who owned the waterworks. —Mr. Brandreth : But they supply water for a certain number of hours each day.—Mr.,Aaron : There is some- times no .water for whole days.—The Sanitary Inspector said many of the water pipes in Weston were corroded up, and if these were replaced and new lengths of main. were laid, and there was a little more pumping, the evil would be remedied.—Mr. Heywood advised Mr. Aaron to get a tank. A certificate of occupation could be given to a house where there was nothing but a supply ef rain water. Therefere he did not see what power the authority had over a house that had a supply from a waterworks.—The Medical Officer said a house could be occupied if there was a sufficient supply nf rain water which they could use.—Mr. Aaron: Without filtering ? —The Medical Offiaer : I Walk the occupants can use their own discretion as to filtering.—Mr. Aaron said one _ half of the houses were not supplied with water, but all the same the Commissioners demanded the rate a quar- ter in advance, and if the money were not paid, a sum- mons was sent. He did not think it was right.—Mr. Heywood : Can you say that this want of supply is injurious to health.—Mr. Aaron : Yes. There was no water to wash out the sewers, or the back yards, or the slop stones or anything e'se. He contended that such a state of thingswas injur ou; to health.—The Clerk said 'if the Waterworks Compi.ny did not carry out their Act Mr- Aaron had a remedy against them.—Mr. Aaron: Has one individual to fight the Company ? He moved that the Inspector examine the houses in We;ton where there was a deficient supply of water, and that he report in a fortnight as to whether it was injurious to health or te4. It was a great wonder they had not had more sickness in the township that; they had.—Mr. Hey- -Wood seconded the motion which was carried

===== 0002601_18931230_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
A TALK WITH Mk. JEROME.

After a while I turned the conversation upon his own work in particular, asking him bow it was he was able, out of his own inner consciousness as it were, to evolve so many curious specimens of humanity,and yet so true to life. " Well," he thoughtfully replied, "it is rather difficult to say off-hand, because though I am always studying- life in the rough, yet I rarely pick out solitary specimens of humans. Things sink in unconsciously, I suppose. I couldn't tell you what a man had on five minutes after he had left me, but after a while, if I thought about him, he would all come back to rue. As to what they say and think—the characters in my books —1 always feel as if I knew what a man would say ; if I see his face lean always tell what he is going to say,for words, modes of expression, are so much the part of a man. Take, for instance, that double character in my Novel Notes,' where a man is a gentleman for one month and an East end blackguard the next month. Whenever I conjured up the two faces of that one man I knew exactly what each would say. Lavater says that you can alter your feelings by your facial muscles. Stick out the jaw,' he says, and you feel determined at once.' I often do that myself. The muscles have a distinct effect on the will power. It simply means that body and mind re-act on one another. Wag a dog's , tail to make him good humoured. If you can make a man laugh against his will, his bad humour goes, dis- persed by the muscles of laughter. You notice this often in the House of Commons. I always enter int* my characters' thoughts and feelings : I can't help my- self. Mr. Solomon in painting my portrait the other day noticed that. I happened to think of a murder in my story ; the savageness of it came into my face, and he had to paint the expression over again when my thoughts were ol a more cheerful nature. I drn't work from my

head, but from my heart. For the time being lam the man lam describing. I could not possibly describe him and his doings in cold blood. Take 'Uncle Podger ' banging the pictures in "Three Men in a Boat.' I actu- ally was that man putting up the pictures, or rather, I was the people standing round him. He was only re- flective. I did not take him from his point of view. I was his disgusted wife, looking on. I dare say he thought the onlookers a pack of fools. My characters really live while lam writing about them. Now they are like people gone abroad—they have dropped out of my life. It is not till I have got two-thiids through my book that I know my new characters. Many authors go back to their old characters. Bret Harto must have loved to go back to IN." " And what class of people do you like best to describe?' "There again it is difficult to say definitely," replied my host, as he lit one of his never- failing cigarettes. "There is a keen study underneath tise surface to be found in the quieter and better edu- cated classes ; bizarre effects in the eccentric strong , characters ; but it is the totally uncultivated who pro- vide the strongest. My great point, I think, is in Mak- ing people talk ; but I don't think you can train your:. self to it. If you have a strong sympathy,generally you can at once get inside a man. You think with him and express yourself with him. Each class has certain sub- tleties. The costermonger, the politician, the society *lncur ; imagine them gathered round one subject, and how differently each of them would view the same thing, each one from his own point of thought. There would be little turns of wording and expression—the reflex of the man's thought, and this, without taking into account accent or grammatical accuracy—merely the beet of the mind."—" Cassell's Family Magazine."

SPRAINS.

NOTICII TO COEIIIISPONDEYTS.

Mr. J. C. Dalton and Mr. Foulkes : The subjects referred • to in your letters Vitt week have been suanently discussed.

GOOD ADVICE FRO3I VIRGINIA.

Sir,—lt has been a source of great pleasure to me to receive an " Examiner" from the old country, for some time past, but at present the pleasure, is, to a degree, marred by the discussion on polemical amenities, &c. Although I am writing from a distance and the discus- sion in question may be finished before my letter roaches you, still I hope you will grant me enough space to insert what few lines I have to write. Ido not intend to enter any discussion. On the contrary I would like this one to cease and so make room for attic': i of more inter- est, to me at least. I tnyself ana well acquainted with Mr. R. Foulkes, and already know his views on more things than this, in connection with Church matters, but I have net the pleasure of knowing Mr. J.C. Dalton. Without doubt both gentlemen, are well versed in their opinions, but when the expression of their opiniens reaches the stage as published in the " Examiner "01 November 25th and December 2nd, it leads to no mater- ial good. Every man has a right to his own religion, and can champion his cause without the display of nonsense. 'Mat each gentleman will shake hands and agree—to differ—is the ardent wish of •

RUNCORNIAN.

Saltville, Virginia, U.S.A.. Dm 18th, 1893.

NEWSPAPERS AND BOOKS FOR THE INFIRMARY.

Sir,—May I appeal to your readers for an object which I teel sure will enlist their sympathy. The Educational and Recreation Committee of the Social Questions Union is anxious to devote its energies to useful public objects Whatever tends to elevate the taste or brighten the dul- ness of life comes within its scope. It has come to our knowledge that a more liberal supply of suitable literature would be acceptable for the use of patients in the Infirmary. It is an object which must appeal to so many that it is needless for me to enlarge upon it. I proceed at once to practical suggestions. We want copies of the weekly papers. Will those who can spare them drop me a line, and I will furnish them with a supply of stamped and directed wrappers, that there may be no trouble in forwarding them to the lefirmary. We also want honks, or money to buy books. I shall be glad to receive any:good books that can be spared—not the vreedings of the library, not worthless or fable books,but healthy, refreshing or amusing books, such as people need during a period of confinement to keep up their spirits and take them out of themselves. Surely some of your readers will be glad to send us some money to pur- chase books of this kind. I can promise that we shall use our best thought and judgment in its disbursement. I may add that the Managing Committee of the Infirm- ary is aware of our project, and has expressed its will- ingness to receive whatever suitable literature we may be able to provide for the patients —I am. yours, Ste., WM. HAMILTON DRUMMOND, 20, Bold-st.,Warrington, Dec. 28, 1893.

===== 0002601_18931230_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BIBLE.

Sir,—" Dissenter" has done precisely what one might have expected him to do. Finding himself confronted .with a tew searching criticisms, that 0 to the very root of his religious principles, and expose their defectiveness, he seeks to evade the difficulty in a manner characteris- tic of the controversial school to which he belongs. I am as familiar, probably, as he himself is. with " Helps to the study of the Bible "—the useful little work to which he refers me—and therefore, I am in a position to say that it does not settle the question as to whether the Bible is composed of all the inspired writings that have ever been penned. In my previous letter, I gave what I conceive to be substantial reason for holding that a number of such writings have perished, and conse- quently, I think I am fully justified in postulating that no one can logically assert the Bible to contain the whole of God's Revelation,unless he can prove,in the first place, that the Word of God, in its entirety, has been reduced to writing, and in the second, that no necessary part of that writing has been lost. Consequent upon this is the very obvious conclusion, that to assert of any Catholic doctrine a want of Scriptural authorisation, does not disprove its claim to a place among those truths which have been divinely revealed. "Dissenter " is certain that his own interpretation of the Bible is the right one ; whence, of couzse, it follows that he is certain also of everyone who has the temerity to differ from him being in the wrong. But then, " Dissenter's" certitude is no evidence that he does rightly interpret the Scriptures ; and considering that Holy Writ contains things hard to be understood, which the unlearned and unstable wrest to their own destruc- tion (2 Pet. 111., 14) and furthermore, that my Wssent- ing friend has received no Divine assurance of that infallibility which he seems to claim for himself, I should be very loth to permit my doctrinal belief to coincide, in all particulars, with his.—Yours, AUG. J. HILTON. 31, Arthur R ad, Nsrth Brixton, London, S.W. December 26, 1893. CHRIST AND THE CATHOLICS. Sir,—The proverb says there is no use' in disputing about tastes. 'these depend so much on a man's en- vironment, and this again is so far beyond his control, that I trust your correspondent " Dissenter "will not take .offence if I avow a preference for the Church's atmosphere of human tenderness and reverence for divine things, and refuse to discuss according to his . taste such matters as our doctrines regarding saints and relics and the taithful departed, or the primacy of St. Peter. With regard to indulgences your correspondent does not seem to know what they mean, and therefore goes astray in his remarks on the passage of scripture referred to. An " indulgence " is a remission of punish- ment ;if this is not " loosing," what is ? The power to " bind and loose" was promised to " the disciples," not as individuals but as a body ; just in the same way as a gift made (say) to " the people " of Warrington be- longs not to individual householders, but to the corpor- ation. Now " the disciples" as a body are the church. Hence it is the church which " binds or looses," and like other organised societies it exercises its authority by means of duly appointed officers, viz., the Pope and other bishops. to grant an" indulgence "is not a very extravagant use of their power, but is certainly in- cluM d within it. " Dissenter " does not say what pro- vision any of the protestant Dissenting bodies make for fulfilling the Apestolic command (Jas. v., 16), to" cm• fess sins one to another "—not "sinfulness "in genes* but actual" sins "committed—and till he has done so, and proved that it is bstter than the provision the Catholic Church makes, I submit he has no right to pose as our critic in this matter. Lastly, let me say again that no Catholic will grant his assumption—it is only an assumption, baseless and incapable of proof—that the Bible, interpreted by the private judgment of the indivi- dual, is the sole rule of Christian faith.—Yours truly, B.L. Dec. 26, 1893.

POLEMICAL AMENITIES.

Beware of false prophets. which come to ymi in !sheep's clothing, tut inwardly are ravening wolves. By their mita

ye shah know ihem." Sir.—Mr. Dalton appears to have called into court and cross-examined the wrong witness. Was that lengthV quotation trom Leay given to prove low tolerant the rationalistic author of "Rati:maiism la Europe" was in his dealings with Romanism ? How willing to say all that Could be said which, if it did not" make her blame- less, would at least. palliate her guilt." Thanking Mr. D. for his very apt, if unconscious, testimony as to the large-nainded toleration ot rationalism. may I ask him to give us some evidence of his acquaintance with any other instances—Hume, Huxley or Spenser? say nothing of the advantages which he might derive from a close study of these authors, he might afford another amusing instance of the citation of witnesses lot. the other side. In the elation naturally following this brilliant display Of forensic ability he proceeds to annihilate me (with laughter) by adducing the Communists as Rationalists ! These poor people, frenzied by had government, and maddened ty seeing the "Eminences" and " Rever- ences" hand and glove with this bad misgovernment— to call these Ratknislists, or even rational, is sufficient evidence of our critic's want of rationalism an his own part. Atheists they might be, largely so by the corrup- tion of his adopted church. Having thus de-vindieated his own claim to either reason or raionalisin, Mr. Dalton begins to rant about "trusting his salvation to poor finite reason, as do your egotistical Rationali&ts." •Oh ! indeed as Rationalists we don't trouble ourselves about an imaginary salvation beirg rendered neeuful by an equally fanciful fall—now quite discounted by ascertained facts. We do not in- tend to give up our dearly bought intellectual freedom at the bidding of a body of Italian shavelings. See what an exhibition of intdlectual degradation these petti- coated holy Eminences have made of their submissive devotee Prof. Mivart ! So! we are to accept dictation On such matters from the infallible source from whence came the command to Galileo to declare the world did not move. But it has moved—to the overthrow of this infallible blonder—and the cleansing of that sink of all Iniquities the Papal states. "By their fruits ye shall know them." As before I say that Rationalism—and Rationalism alona—botk within and without thechurches, what keeps them from falling to pieces by their own Corruption. All human institutions—and I know of no other—are liable—nay certain—to become foul and noisome by the lapse of time. •But try to take Anglican- ism, Romanism, or any other 'ism to pieces to cleanse it,

)

_

and see what a hubbub their "Reverences" will raise I But, no! these polluting accretions must go as until the whole fabric comes to the ground by the weighs of its own iniquities. And now, sir, I may say that some of my earliest re- collections are of an old Catholic lady whose school I at- tend, d—whose patience I tried, and whose image even yet; has a large niche in ray heart. I have many friends, of the same faith, whose good opinion I should be sorry to lo,e ; but what I hold to be the truth is more sacred than friendship or the pleasant recollections of youth. The blood-stained pagers of ecclesiastic history need only ' to be read with intelligence to convince any reasonable mind that mere religious opinion is not worth fighting for, not worth, persecuting for, not worth dying for. Meanwhile men are noose and more imbued with the salutary idea that reason—the best of God's gifts—is a safer guide through life than the dogmatism that burnt its brothers and drowned its witches. Mr. D. carefully avoids telling us what it is that prevents us from doing , the lattereven now ?—Yours, Arc., JO ON PEERS.

THE SUNDAY SCHOOL UNION.

- - - Sir,—Your last issue contained a statement ef a special rale of the new Warrington Sunday School Union which all schools within is five mile radius are invited to join. Now, as there is probably only Cairo-street Sun- day School within this area that %maid d+cline on prin- ciple to accept this Mediteval system of creeds.l presume it is meant to keep that heretical institution out of this select fold. It appears that to gain admission, the applicant must " Hold the doctrine of the Trinity ;" " The atonement of our Lord Jesus Christ ;" " The regenerative influence of the Holy Spirit ;" " And that all scripture is given by inspiration of God." Why the doctrine of an eternal Hell is left out, rather surprises me ! Twenty years ago it would have been considered indispensable. But a little light has dawned on the minds of some orthodox leaders even in Warrington, who can hardly reconci!e the belief in a God who consigns to eternal damnation with the Fatherly and being God ot Jesus Christ. It seems as if the principles enunciated in the Sermon on the Mount, and in the grand yet simple and practical teachings of the Master in the Gospels, are not in them- selves good enough without the addition of propositions, one at least of which, its professed believers admit they cannot understand. - .

The defenders of the " Trinity " still do what I who was trained to believe it did nearly 50 years ago—they take refuge behind the fence ol " a great mystery" when pressed closely by sound argument. Then, as belief in the Atonement is insisted on, it might have been worth while to define which doctrine of Atonement is the all- important one. For I have known about 20 different ones propounded at various times by orthodox divines. Now I have heen hearing and reading Christ's matchless Parable of the Prodigal Son for a lifetime, and I defy any reasonable man to find a trace of this doctrine there. It is remarkable, too, that the revised version does not contain the name Atonement. In the old version it only appears once (Rom. 5 and 11), but in the revised ver- sion it has been replaced, by orthodox revisers, by the better word "Reconciliation." So now our friends have to go outside the pages of the Bible to find a name for the doctrine. The word " Trinity " never had a place even in the old version. _ _ . .

As to the last section of this wonderful rule—" That ! all Scripture is given by inspiration of God," I would ask if the belief in witches, and that they should not be ' suffered to live, is a part of this inspiration? Does this inspiration rightly charge the Divine Being with com- manding the perpetration of most atrocious cruelties on helpless women and children and even on their cattle? Is there no difference in the inspiration of the Psalmist when he said, in reference to Babylon, " Happy shall he be who taketh thy little ones and dasheth them against the stones," and the sublime prayer of Jesus, "Father forgive them for they know not what they do?" Yt t these and many more equally incongruous illus- trations are includicl in the Scriptures,which are said to be all given by inspiration of God ,and to be accepted as such by those who would be members of this union. • ...Surely, in face of such narrow exclusiveness we are Instilled in re-echoing the words of the Master,"Ye know not what manner of spirit ye are of." Such a constitu- tion would have excluded from communion even in good works some of the greatest intelle;:ts and noblest spirits that have lived to bless and elevate humanity. John Milton, our great epic poet, would have been shut out, for he wrote a treatise against the doctrine of the Trinity. Florence Nightingale, whose noble self-sacrifices in the Crimea drew forth from this nation a mighty tribute of affectionate admiration would not be orthodox enough for this little Union. Or, coming nearer home, men like Philip Carpenter, Richard Pitcher, and many others, whose names have been household words in all Christian activities in this town, would be counted unworthy to jein with their fellow townsmen in helping on the work they so dearly loved. In conclusion, much as I disagree with the constitu- tion of this Union, I will give five pounds to its funds if any of its promoters can find me a single genuine text of Scripture which gives either the name or is descrip- tive of the doctrine of the Trinity.—Yours, T. B. December 21, 1893.

===== 0002601_18931230_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
A DEFENCE OF THE COMMUNE &c.

Sir,—Religion from the beginning has be.sn the essen- tial part of the machinery used to govern a:' nations, and in all cases the result has been the same. Turn your eyes to any of them—the Mohammedan, the Jewish, tne Roman Catholic, the Greek Church, all these religions have the same object in view, that is to absorb all before them for the special benefit of the clergy. In proportion to the power acquired, these religions work mischief to the people. In our midst,we have men who make them- selves ever conspicuous by the earnest way in which they champion their particular pet religion. Our friend, Mr Dalt MI. is one of those. Last week he turned to blam- ing the Paris Commune for the shooting of Monseigneur Darboy. A revolution is a fracticidal war, and as such always to be regretted and deplored, but when we would elect ourselves as judges of historical occurrences we ought to be impartial in our verdicts. During the Com- munist rising, prisoners were made on both sides and the army of Versailles,under the command of MacMahon, invariably shot the Communists without any trial. Now, the leaders of the Comisune seat word to Mscllahon that if the Communist prisoners were shot, the Com- munists would shoot the hostages, and they did so. How many innocent men and women did MacMahon's soldiers shoot ? Thousands. Mr. Dalton would do much better service to the Church of Rome by letting her alone. ,Her history is a long record of tyranny and cruelty from the very moment that her prelates got into power. The Church of Rome was a failure in France as a governing machine. The French Revolution of 1789 and the Commune of 1871 were terrible national convulsions ' caused by Governments over which the Church of Rome had an all-powerful influence, but the deeds of blood of those revolutions stand as nothing compared with the centuries of tortures and misery under the sway of the Mother Church.

I would ask our friend Mr. Dalton to take notice of the last effort on the part of the Pope to use his influence in these realms on behalf of the aristocracy. I rn-am. when lie sent Monseigneur l'ersico over to Ireland imme- diately after the visit of the Duke of Nerfolk to Rome. Monseigneur Persico come over to Ireland to persuade the Irish Catholic hierarchy t 3 use their influence over their clergy and flocks and try to break up the political agi- tation known as the Land League. Although he failed in his efforts, it is worth while to remember on what side Rome would fight if she could. It is as well to say that religions, being human institu- tions, are all faulty. Those who are oppressed to-day would be oppressors to-morrow if they could. Look at the tyranny of our extreme Sabbatarians and the Knights of the Punua. Those 80-called Puritans are the very people who would rule with an iron rod if they could; they are the ones who do not forget to rule when- ever opportunity is offered. After expressing my opinion of various religions, I have no doubt there will be found those who would call me an infidel, unbeliever, atheist, and say that Socia- lists are against religion in any form. It is not so. We Socialists would observe the golden rule : Do unto others as you wish they would do unto you. Let there be liberty all round, freedom of speech,freedona of the Press,liberty of conscience. Mr. Dalton says in your last issue that Socialists would erase the name of God from the walls of the schools ; that we would not have religious education in our schools ; that the French Government have pre- scribed the Roman Catholic teaching Orders and so on. The duty of government is to hold an even balance, and as much as Mr,Dalton grumbles at the Christian brothers losing state patronage,much more would I a shriek if the government were to promote the Jewish faith. Yet the French Government has no more right to pay the Chris- tian brothers with State money than to pay Jewish teachers. Let education be free and compulsory and strictly secular for five days per week. There are seven days in the week,one of which can be entirely devoted to religious education for the children of those who wish for it and are willing to pay for it. We Socialists would nut oppress our fellows but would strenuously resist oppres- sion. The time was—when I went to school in France-- that all our school books had to be revised by the Roman Catholic Archbishop of Cambrai. In those days Roman Catholics were state manufactured in France, but,thank God, that day is gone. Then a man had no option.there was only one school in the village, and that school was state-supported and priest-ridden. So many hours a day were stolen from our time and devoted to Roman Catholic education. Religious education of the Roman Catholic persuasion was compulsory to the exclusion of more use- ful knowledae. History and geography were almost entirely neglected, and Roman Catholic catechism took up precious time. I want Mr. Dalton and others to acknowledge this much. Now, in France, education is free and compulsory up to fourteen years of age. The schools are State supported. and entirely secular educe: tion is provided on a generous and ample scab.. But in France there. is liberty_ .of _conscience,

and no one prevents Roman Catholic parents name bringing up their children in t ieir own faith The only thug that Mr. Dalton can complain of is that the Church of Rome has lost the control of the education of Young Fraqce. except in as far as the parents may wish it. Roman Catholics, in France, are not persecuted. —Yours truly, A. BROUCDEMITE.

THE EXAMINER, SATURD AY. DECEMBER 3O, is.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

Warrington footballers havej should imagine,enjoyed a nserry Christmas. At any rate they have had plenty of play.and they are said never to be happier than when engaged in an exposition of the game. On Saturday they made a journey to Lancaster, and frightened the senior team of the town by only allowing them to squeeze their victory into a margin so narrow that the most verbose among them could not make a brag of it. John Bate was the only noticeable absentee from the Warrington ranks ;his place was tilled by Clark. Bee- som made a g•od show at back again—but, where has " Sureloot " got to ? Surely the inmth is up which it was 'aid would cover the whole of his absence?

On Boxing day the team met Kendal Flernets, at Wilderspool. They won by six points to tour. But oh! what a time they were having. Indeed, some people said they were "having "the spectators ; but there are always those who will cavil and growl at seeing others enzaged in that thrice glorious occupation of enjoying themselves. Both teams were representative ones, and play was much enjoyed by the large holiday " gate" which was present. Good play came especially from the three-quarter ranks and the forwards. At the inter- val, Kendal were leading by threz points, and for a time matters did not wear the most roseate glow for Warring- ton. However, a special spurt did all that was required, and the locals were eventually entitled to sing "See the c.ucquering Heroes come."

The A team has not been heard of very much of late, but on Saturday they favoured the Warrington public with a match with the A team from Lancaster. They have evidently become rather disorgani.sed with their pro- longed holiday, having only played one match during the past seven weeks. But they proved' equal to the task of beating Lancaster. The Northerners, however, were a sturdy lot, and required some beating, in fact " Jack was almost as good as his master." There was very little to /kite in the match, it being a very straggl- ing affair from beginning to end. Barber, the Stockton Heath threequarter, was a favourite with the crowd, and it is a great pity that he had not a little more work to do, for un the only occasion when .he had a fair chance, he darted ever the line through the midst of the Lancastrians.

Lally and Lawless at half back were agile enough,but they did not pass with sufficient freedom, and thus spoiled numerous chances. Lally was the best of the two, and had he been a sprinter he would have scored several times. Boscow, who again plared for the A team, waa in very good form,particularly in the kick- ing department, and were he a little more speedy, he would have almost been " hiinself again." It would be a difficult matter to say which of the opposing forwards were the best ; they were about evenly matched. The score was, strange to say, exactly the same as that of the first team on the following Tuesday against Kendal Hornets, viz., two tries to a dropped goal, so that the Warrington ground is apparently a favourable place for the northern tcams to exhibit their cleverness in drop- ping goals.

===== 0002601_18931230_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
ANTECEDENTS.

Mr. J. T. Bate, better known in football circles as " Jack Bate," who holds the distinguished position of captain of the first team, is the subject of our sketch this week. He first commenced playing about ten years ago with the Old Latchford True Blues, and in the fol- lowing season he joined the White Star club, of Town's End. But his stay with this club was of short duration for soon afterwards he commenced playing in the 2nd team of the Warrington Football club. He was appointed sub-captain and afterwards captain of this team, and during his stay in the" A" team some years afterwards he fulfilled the same positions. Strange to say he has now occupied exactly the same positions in the first team. This it may be said is unprecedented in local football history, for " Jaaa " is the only player who has con- trolled the three teams in all these capacities.

As is well known, J. T. Bate's forte is at half tack ; in fact he has never played in any other position in the team. His compeer at half-back is' Smack "Bate, and they are c..nsidered the best couple in Lancashire in that position, and to their quickness and alertness many victories of the Warringson team are due. This year J. T. Bate's abilities have received due recognition by the county committee. He has played in two trial matches and was placed on the reserve in the match against Cumberland. His services, however, were not required. But in the match against Yorkshire he was included in The Lancashire fifteen, and played a sterling game at halt-back accompanied by his name-sake. That the County Committee were satisfied with his play is shown by his selection again in the county team against •Che- shire to be played to-day (Saturday).Warrington players it should be stated, have had a good share in County honours lately, for in several matches they have com- posed one fifth or the county team.

===== 0002601_18931230_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

__- THE P.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE CFIURCII.

There was again a very large attendance at the usual weekly P.S.A. meeting in Wye life Church on Sundae afternoon. The chair was occupied by Mr. .1. Mills who in a short address spoke on" Nobleness " Mr. R. Hen- shall delivered the address, and spoke of what he con- sidered "The main element in national greatness." He contended that a country's greatness depended on the moral quality of its people, and as an illustration he contrasted the condition of the nation under the pro- tectorate with what it had been previously. He re- minded the young men that a great deal depended on them in respect to the future of this country. Miss Nekton sang two solos in an admirable manner. The Rev. J. Yonge,Mr. T. Wallington, and Mr. D. Owes also took part in the service.

BOLD-ST. WESLEYAN CFIAPEL.

- _ The pulpit at the shove place of worship was occupied on Sunday morning bc the Rev.G. D. Mason. and in the evening by the Rev. J. Kirtlan. During the evening service Miss Rolle sang as a solo "He shall feed His flock like a shepherd," in a finished style, and the choir also rendered the anthem 0 Zion that brim:est vnd tidings." On the morning of Christmas Day, the Rev. G. D. Mason preached in the chapel, taking for his text Matthew 2, 1 and 2. Fie said that it would be well to pause in the midst of their thrtrging duties to reflect for a while on the meaning and purpose of the great event of the Incarnation of Jesus Christ in the history of the world. There were grouped together different representa- tive characters in the first and last scenes of our Lord's earthly life. For example, at the crucifixion there WAS the indifference of the Roman soldiers dividing His gar- ments among themselves, and, also the centurion, who said, "Truly this is the Son of God." There were also 1 the disciples, who said that they trusted that He had been the Saviour of Israel. There were still representa- tives of these various characters at the present day. In the same manner.at His hirth,there was the adoration of the shepherds. They hid in Herod the type of those who were malignantly hostile to Christianity. In the wise men from the East, they had the type of the earnest seekers after Christ. Tradition said that the wise men were three kings. That they were not ordinary persons is shown by the fact of the notice taken of their arrival in Jeru- salem, for the whole city WAS disturbed, and Herod at first received them courteously. The particular form their philosophy took was the study of the stars, and they no doubt expected to find on their arrival in Jeru- salem general rejoicing in the birth of Christ, but they were not repelled by finding Him in such humble sur- roundings and conditions in Bethlehem. There- were those who said that there was sufficient-in the world to satisfy everyone, and that they might, if they would, find the realisation of their hopes in the common -things of this life. But though a person had everything he hoped or wished for in this life, there was a something that could net be s.tistied, and would not lse satisfied until he found Christ and worshipped Him. Army of the places of worship were comfortably constructed, but suitable arrangements were not made for outward re- verence. He observed that in the worship of these wise men there was reverence and outward homage, and he thought that this was a lesson to thris of the present day, to give their whole heart and soul to the service of the Master. During the service Mr. Harrison played upon the organ" He shall feed His flock lika a shepherd.'

DUKE ALFRED AND HIS ANNUTIE'S.

SEMI-OFFICIAL DECLARATION.

Cormlto, THURSDAY. —The official "Cobwieer Zeitung" to-day, referring to the recent debate in the House of I Commons regarding the allowance to the Duke of Coburg, remarks :—" Newspapers are in error in imagin- ing that there is any question of an annual vote in re- gard to the Duke's allowance. The two Acts of Parlia- ment referred to in the House of Commons could only have been modified or rescinded. If that were the case one of these Acts were disposed of by the renunciation of the Duke, while the other is upheld and remains hence- forth beyond the range of discussion by the British Par- liament. The first Ant provides tor the allowance to the Duke in his capacity as a lirstish Prince, while the second granted him a further allowance for his house- hold on the occasion of his marriage, and is indissolubly bound up with provision made for the Duchesi in the event of her widowhood. As regards the latter, the Duke could make 110 r:mimeiation, since such a step would have seriously prejudiced rights conferred upon the Duchess in the event of her becoming a widow. BY , treaty between Great Britain and Russia, moreover, the ' Duke's household has existed for several decades. His Royal Highnesa has spent large sums on structural im- provements at Clarence House, and has had to keep 1 numerous officials and servants who cannot well be dis- missed now. It should also be considered that the Duke as heir to the late Ibike, had no grants for Saxe-Coburg Gotha. If, therefore, the British Parliament recognised the Duke of Edinburgh's English obligations of anterior date, the German Press need not be annoyed because the Delte's German allowances, which only begin now, exclusively benefit his German pentirin and the people of Coburg Gotha, especially as his Royal Highness, as heir to the throne of Coburg, has for the last twenty years defrayed out or his own resources the cost fif maintaining a residence in Germany.

A WARRINGTONIAN IN TLI.E FAR WEST.

We have received the following letter which, we believe, will be perused with interest by many of our readers :

The Pieaaant, 221, Marion-at., Seattle, U. S. A., Dee. 1893._ . .

Mr. Editor.—Once more I take great pleasure in writing to my friends in Warrington, through your paper, as I heard from several that they would like to hear from me again. I would like to inform my friends in the first place that I catered the tnatri- monial state on the 20th November last. I was married by the Rev. Hugh Pedley, of the Congrega- tional Church, W innipeg, to Miss Rean Toinp:sins, daughter of George Tompkine, Lietowel, Ontario. Ysiu will see by the above address that at preient I am not in the prairie capital (Winnipeg), as I left that city immediately after the marriage ceremony by the West- bound train, 10.30 a.m., for the Pacific coast and then down to Seattle in Uncle Sam's domains. Things were looking pretty dull in Maniteba for everything was at a standstill and will be until May. So I concluded to see a little of the country on my wedding trip, and at the same time escape the cold weather of the Manitoban winter. I fixed on Seatle, Washington territory, for my destination, that city being about 1,600 miles a little to the south-west of Winnipeg. It is my trip out there that I would like to speak about, and the beautiful scenery which lies along tee Canadian Pacific Railway to the coast. I don't think it could be surpassed. anywhere for mountain scenery, neither in this country, nor any other for grandeur, etc. Well, at 10-30 a.m., on the 20th (Nlonclay), we grt on board the tourist car, which is a little palace in itself, for con- venience, and comfort, and we are shown to our berth, by the polite and mannerly,neero poi ter,who has charg• of the car. I would like to say a word or two in praise of these coloured porters, as I find nearly everyone are down on the derkiee, and I don't for the life of me,,see why they should be, as they are no worse than white men, and I would by far prefer the darkies to scores of white men I have come in cestitact with, while rolling around this country. The railroads of Canada, and United States, employ only negroes, as porters, for they are by far more polite and oleiling than the average white man, and I " kinder think" they are myself. You ask them to do anything for you, and it's done, as if it were a pleasure to them, and you cannot help but get on friendly terms with them,as they go about their work singing, and smiling, or chatting away to you. They are mostly all jolly, good hearted fellows, and no differ- ence in them at all—except that they they have the mis- fortune to have a black skin. They are sober and in- dustrious, and what more is wanted? I have seen scores of niggers, but never seen one drunk yet. But I had better get on with my letter, as I arms taking up too much space in your paper. I'm afraid. The train leaves Winnipeg, on time, and with many handsnakings, and goodbye's, we are off. As we know we shall see nothing for the day but the snow-covered prairies and having nothi•g to do, we soon make the acquaintance of our fellow passengers in the car. Before night we arc all at home with one another, and might have been acquainted for months. We get amongst the endless wheat fields, in the afternoon, now covered with snow, and about 9 p.m. we reach Indian Head, the headquarters of the celebrated Bell Farm, which con- tains 100 square miles of lamed. I should have liked to have passed here in the day time, and no snow to be seen; for they say you can see scores of the farm labourers cottages' dotting the plains as far as the eye can see. The furrows on this farm are usually ploughed four miles long ; and to plough one furrow outward, and another returning, is a half day's work for a man and team. Shortly after nine o'clock, the porter comes and makes our beds tor us, and then we turn in and find it hard to get to sleep, as the jolting of the cars is not very pleasant, but being tired we are soon in the land of dreams. About 2 am, we reach Regina, and as the train steams into the station my wife and I are out of our berth and on the platform of the car on the lookout for our friends, whom we expect to meet here, and we are not disappointed, for the train has barely stopped, when my wife is pulled to the platform by her sisters, and brother-in-law, the latter being Emigration Agent for the Province. We are not allowed much time, to see our friends, for in live minutes, the train begins to leave the station, and we lose sight of them in the dark- ness. Regina, I may say, Is A pretty place, the governor of the Province residing there ; it is also the headquarters of the North-west mounted police, a military organize- tion,nuinberine 1,000 men,who are stationed at intervals over the North-west and along the border to look after the Indians and to preserve order. When we leave Regina, it does not take us long to get into our berth again, and to sleep souadly, the rest of the night. Next morning (Tuesday) we are all up early, and every one bustling and preparing their breakfast. Soon after breakfast, we see one of the Canadian Agricultural Company's farms, which covers 10,000 acres. 'this Company have several of these farms, in different districts, and altogether they have about 1.05,000 acres of land. Each farm contains an average ot 10,000 actes,and it is supposed they have about 4,000 acres under cultivation at each point. You can judge what some of the farms are like out here for size. In the afternoon we are in the esaching country, as now and again' we see great banes of cattle grazing and numerous corrals; and now and agaen a branding corral along the track. Towards night everything begins to change, and soon we are amongst the foothills, and as we look out of tho car windows we become more interested, as we are now near the Rockies, and everyone is eager to see the magnificent scenery, so much praised. Some of the passengers are so interested that they decide to keep awake all night ; for fear they should miss any of the scenery. As for myself and wife, we were tired, and so we retired for the night with the intention of rising early in the morning. At six a.m. (Wednesdayl I turned out and seeing that we are climbing the Rockies,' immediately den my over- coat and cap,—as it is pretty cold, and get out on the platform of the car so as to get a better view of things, and Was enjoying myself immensely taking in the sights. We had passed the 13anff hot springs in the night to my sorrow, as I should have liked to have seen the place. The first place I SAW in the mountains was Lag- pan, close to Mount Stephen, where we see the first glaciers and in twenty minutes we pass that mountain which is the summit of the Rockies. The scenery now makes you gaze open mouthed, awe struck, at the ter- rible height of the mountain on the left,and we shudder as we see the river on the right in the valley, a thous- and feet below us. All around can be seen scores of peaks. On the slope of Mount Stephen on its shoulder, almost overhead, we can see the shining green ice of a glacier, supposed te be 800 feet thick, which is slowly pressing forward over the steep cliff,. The glacier looked as if it were on fire when the sun shone on it. The train stopped a little further on at Field. Here we have a chance to stretch our limbs and look around for forty minutes.and thea gives us a chance to replenish our lunch baskets. At Field is to be seen a charming little h ate!— the Mount Steehen House—managed by the railway company. It is situated at the base of Mount Stephen and facing Mount Field. This is one of the prettiest places in the Rockies; as you look down the valley you have a goad view of the Ottertail Mountains and, on the right the Tan Horne range. The forty minutes' grace are tip, and the train moves on, and soon we cross a bridge that spans the Ottertail river—l think—that flows down the western slopes of Kicking Horse Pens and rushes into the lower Kicking llorse carillon. Now and again we se. streams of water rushing over the sides of the mountains which dazzle our eyes, as the sun shines on them. When we get out of the pass we are in the cainjon, and here we see the running at is tremendous speed, twisting and twirling below, and with the roar of the water and train com- bined we are nearly deafened. Suddenly we are out of the caution and Golden is reached on the Columbia River. This is a mining town, as we can see the mines plainly along the track now and again. There are see's: ral geld and silver mines in this district. The rivee is navigable and steamers run weekly to Kootenay Valley another great mining district. The next place we stop at is Donald, and here we have the Pacific time, so I have to pat my watch back two hours since leaving Winnipeg. The Selkirk Mountains are separated from the Rockies hereby the Columbia River and we are soon across it and into the gorge. The railway now appears to be on shelves, as it clings to the sides of the moon- taine, and it would make you shiver to look down below. As we leave the gorge behind we are amoae the Selkirk Mountains ; and again we are surrounded by high peaks. The mountains are all covered with great spruce, fir, and cedar trees, and above the clouds we can see the tops covered with snow. The cascades we pass now look splendid, as the water dashes in a white foam below. The track along here runs pretty steep, 116 feet to the mile, and I believe the company had a pretty hard time in building their line here, owing to the water rushing down the slopes into the narrow gorges,through which the railroad is built. Soon we are crossing over the cantilever bridge bich spans Stoney Mashonas, a large but feeble Bantu nation, represent- Creek. This bridge is 295 feet eeeive the creek, and it Mg the aboriginal element in the region between the makes us shudder to think what a smash we sheuld have Limpopo and the Zambesi, but during the present cen- if we slipped the track here. tury driven by the intruding Metal-ells to the hilly We are now in time most dangerous parts of the northern plateau, which from them takes the name of mountains for we are going thrsugh numerous snow Mashonaland, and which is now being occupied by sheds that are built over the track. These Save the British settlers under the Chartered South Africa Corn- company a ereat expensteand prevent the track from pany. The Mashonas have eagerly accepted the British getting blocked. When there is a snow slide, it glides protectorate as their best defence against the pluederine over the shed. amid into the valley below, not touching Matabili hordes; hut, being regarded by the Waal-ens as the track in the least,and I don't think the biggest snow their legitimate prey, the standing feud between the two slide iinaeined could dislodge them. At we go through peoples has already brought the white settlers into colli- Rcesers Pass. between Hermit mnuntain and Macdonald Arm with the Matabili, and the settlement will continue mountain, the sides of which are bare and etraight as to he threatened by these fierce Zulu warriors until their an arrow, we wonder how it is tle.y don't topple inward, military organisation is broken. The Mashonas are It is supposed these two mountaine were once united, themselves an inoffensive, industrious people, who till and split apart by an eruption or something, and barely their lands with great care, raise cotton crops, with I left room for the railway to creep between. Leaving which they spin and weave coarse textiles, show much the Pass, we soon arrive at the Op of the Selkirks. Here skill in basket-work and in the manufacture of iron Iwe See the Great Glacier, a great mass of ice on the implements. They have also long worked in a primitive I mountains' extending as far as the eye can reach. The way at the rich alluvial and quartz gold digginee of the mountain, Sir Donald, is now seen and is said to rise plateau ; hut they have been greatly reduced by the 100 re than a mile and a half above the track and almost periodical raids of the Matabili, their number having straight.
