On Bank Holiday the Athletic members and their friends of the Warrington and Wigan Postal Staffs met at Wigan for the purpose of having a friendly football match. The visiting contingent from War- rington arrived about two o'clock and at ,once ad- journed to the scene of mimic warfare, the football field. The teams were as follows :—W arrington— goal, Wrench ;Backs. Goodfellow and Dykes ; half backs : Reynolds, Hewitt, Oxley ; forwards, McDonald, Gardner, Bennion, Hallows, and Jones. Wigan—Goal, John Marsh ; Backs, Brindle and Charnock ; half backs, Langton, Morgans, Edgar; forwards Lyons, Ciayton, Urinston, Lonsdsle, Meadow. Wigan won the toss,and kicked off. Even play for the first ten minutes even- tually resulted in a spirited rush by the Wiganers on the opposing custodian. However a barren corner caused the return of the ball to midfield when a fine bit of combination on the part of the forwards gain caused pressure in the Warringtonians territory, and the ball was put outside. The tributary of this Douglas now became a factor in the game, for it overflowed its banks, and wasted its waters and sweet- ness (!) in several of the hollow spots on the field, thus making the " going " very heavy an the ball's motion rather erratic. Several players appe'ared to rather like it, judging from the amount of mud adhering to them. R. Urmston after some tine play, in which the ball was taken well up the lield,put the ball just over the bar. The kick out relieved the visitors' anxiety. Wigan again pressed, some fine back play being exhibited by both home and visiting backs. A corner to Warrington re- sulted in the bell takiag another trip up the field, and after a most exciting bit of play Langton struck the post with a " beauty." The ball was now put through by Charnock from a free kick, but the ball, however, not being played, no goal resulted. The game having been very fast up to this a little rtrategy was adopted by each side, and kicking out became quite fashionable for a time. The desire to shine, however, got the better of this, and thee visitors came down with a rush, which was stopped by Brindle. Some very fine play on the left wing by the visiting for- wards was checked by Lyons, and the honnesters began to press once more. But the " sphere " could not be put through the space provided for it. Half time arrive& with no score to either side. Upon the game being resumed the visitors went of with a rattle and McDonald missed a beauty right in the goal mouth. They kept up the pressure for some time but the back play was too good to be trifled with and Brindle putting in a splendid run placed the ball in a dangerous position for the visi- tors' citadel. After some even play a most exciting run was . put in by the borne team, but Lonadale failed to score. A desperate scrimmage, right in front of goal, eventually proved the downfall of the visitors, the winning shot fallitc( to I.lrinstion just seven minutes before time. The game now became very fast, but claims for hands against both sides gave relief. Event- ually Wigan were returned as winners Of a very fast and exciting game by one goal to nil. After the football match the members and friends adjourued to Mr. H. Harrison's "'I he Honeysuckle " Poolstock, where 57 sat down to an excellent dinner, to which ample justice was done. The cloth being removed the usual loyal and patriotic- toasts were given, and responded to, and an excellent programme of " Music, mirth, and mimicry "was given by the following. Selec- tion from " Bohemian Girl " Messrs. Wilfrid and Wal- ter Chamberlain; toast ."The Queen " proposed by Mr. Heaton. Mr. James Edgar next gave a song" Me Heart bowed down." 'then followed the toast by Mr. T. Gerrard, "The President," who in a very apt and appro- priate speech showed bow and why the president was so highly esteemed, both by the club and the oficials gen- erally. The toast was ably responded to by Mr. Char- nock the captain of the club. After this Mr. Butler sang The Diver." The visitors were next "toasted "by Mr. Butler who in a very short speech expressed his satisfaction at the manner in which the visitors played. The toast was responded to by Mr. Goodfellow captain of the Warrington team. Mr. Siinm then in his usual jolly manner gave the assembly a treat with his snag, " You that are married." The" Wolf" was very ably rendered by Mr. R. Heaton, who in response to a raost enthusiastic encore gave "The Village Blacksmith." The selection " Alice where art , thou:" was given by Messrs. Wilfrid and Walter Chan-. berlain. The toast," Our Football Club," was ?ropf.,sed. Mr. J. Oniens who spoke ol the pride 'eirUcfi he took in the same. He spoke of the gm:at-benefits derived from manly exercise in the sAnape of football. This was res- ponded to by Mr. trindle. Ur. Goodfellow, of War- rington, recite,d " A dry subject." Next followed a song by M. K. Ileaton, " Father O'Flynn" which was en- cored most enthusiastically. A violin solo given by Wilfrid Chamberlain followed by the song " There is a }lower that bloometh," by Mr. John Kellett There were afterwards submitted a song " A Medley," Mr. Clayton ; song, "They're after me," by Mr. Emson, of Warrington, which was greatly enj eyed ; violin solo, " Guillatne Tell " (by special desire), Mr. Wilfrid Cham- berlain accompanied on the piano by Mr. Walter Cham- berlain ; song, " The parson and the clerk," by Mr. G. B. Chamberlain, which created great laughter : piano solo, "Thee Lays of Argyle," by Mr. W. Chamberlain. Theusual vote of thanks and" Auld Lang Syne,"brought an enjoyable evening to a close. The company then made their way to the station accompanied by the greater part of the Wigan officials. Cheers and counter cheers were given and it was altogether an exemplification of "After the ball."

touching the re. Ica,' hen spanning the crevices,and when we are at the bottom we look up and see the other tracks, and wonder how the train managed to get down there. Coining out if the carnim we are amongst the mines again, arid we see the enormous trees for which British Columbia is well known. Seen we get tired of the mountains and turn in for the night, but not to sleep much, as the cars roll worse than ever, and we are run- ning over dangerous places. Next morning (Thursday), when I turn out I see the Fraser River. well known for its salmon; which we cross. The scenery around here is beautiful, lsut we cannot take much interest in it as we are busy packing up and getting ready to leave the cars. At Mission Juaction we change cars, and as some of our riends go south from here, we say goodbye and the train takes us westward. We see now numerous tree stumps of enormous size all along the. track, and at noon the train arrives in Vancouver. On leaving the train, all is hurry and bustle to reach the steamboat which we have to take to carry us over to Victoria,the capital of British Columbia, situated on Vancouver Island. We have a pretty rough time crossing the bay,and it makes some of the passengers look rather sheepish over the sides of the steamer. At eight o'clock at night, we reach Victoria, and we could see they had had a snow storm, and As it was now raining, it was not fit to move around. If you did, you would get over the shoe, tops in water. There was nothing for it hut to take a cab to get to the steamer,that ran between Victoria and Seattle, and with the driver putting on speed we managed to catch the steamboat as she was ready to put out of the 'do3k. Next morning (Friday) we find ourselves at Seattle, on Uncle Sam's territory, completely tired out, and hav- ing secured furnished rooms, we settled ourselves down for a good rest, as we had no desire to look around the city that day. The next day we looked around and found that the city is rather a pretty looking place. It is situate on the hill side, facing the sound (Poiget Sound). Numerous steamboats run from here to differ- ent cities along the coast, and ships enter from all parts and there is always a fair amount of shipping in the docks. They have splendid cable cares here for climbing the steep hills, and au excellent electric street car ser- vice. I find times rather dull here at present, as it is throuehout the United States.ancl will be until the tariff questien is finished with. The climate is very mild ; it rains on an averaee of four days in the week, but you can always see the green grass, that's one blessing. I don't know if I shall settle down hero or not, but I will try it for awhile, and if it agrees with my health, and there is plenty of work here, I may stay. I would then see what difference there is under the Stars and Stripes and the Union Jkck. _ _

Mr. Editor, lam eur! am tilting' up too much spice in your paper, which I receive every week from home ; but nevertheless I hope you will publish this letter, arid I hope that iny friends will not criticise the way it is com- posed, as I don't profess to be anything at this kind of work. My wife and I are in good health at present, so I guess now Iliad better conclude, wishing you the coin- Orients of the season, and also all my old friends in Warrington, the same Iron an old Warringtonian.

- JAS. G. HOWARD. Formerly of Winwick-road, Warrington.

WARRINGTON AND WIGAN POSTAL

OFFICIALS RE-UNION.

SPEAKMAN OF WIINCOTii.

DEATH AND FUNERAL OF MR. T. W.

Mr. Thomas Wright Speakman, eldest son of Mr. Philip Speakman, J.P., C.C., died at the residence of his parents, The Mount, Higher Runcorn, about half past seven on Christmas night. Eleven weeks ago, Mr. Speakman was laid prostrate by an attack of typhoid fever, of a very severe type: When this disease. had been successfully battled with, other complications ensued; and despite the skilful and assiduous attentions of Dr. McDougall, who had the benefit of consultations with Dr. Glynn, of Liverpool, and the most patient nursing; he passed away at the time stated. The name of Speakman is a household word in Runcorn, and when the news of the mournful event became circulated on Tuesday morning the deepest sympathy was expressed for'the bereaved parents and relatives. Mr. and Mrs. Speakman have heen the recipients Of hundreds of letters of condolence ,couched in terms calculated to assuage the poignancy of their grief. Although only in his 32nd wear, Mr. Speak- man had achieved a reputation in commercial circles throughout the country of which a much older and ex- perienced man might have been proud. After complet- ing his education at the Liverpool Institute, Mr. Speak- man.was placed at a desk in his father's office, and dis- played an aptitude for business that caused him to be given a partnership in the firm some years later. The firm then assumed the title of Messrs. P. Speakman and Son. He entered into the business with all the ardour of his energetic nature, and the already extensive field of the firm's operations, was widened and extended, and the ramifications af the business spread throughout the United Kingdom and Ireland. His honesty and integ- rity, and straightforward dealing, combined with much tact and a; genial disposition made him highly popular with those with whom he came in contact; and as be- fore remarked, he had an enviable reputation in com- mercial circles. y the employers of the Arm, he was held in high respect and esteem, and by his death they have lost a kind and considerate master. In private he was held in the highest regard, and was blessed by an extensive circle of friends. Unlike his father who devotes much of his valuable time in work for the pnblic weal, Mr." Tom" took no part whatever in public life, but he was an active worker in the church. For some years be had identified himself with St. Michael's church and he had held the office of vicar's warden for the past two years. He was also a manager of Newtown School. That a young man of such capabi- lities, at d with such a proaperous career in prospect, should have been cut oft in his early manhood is a loss to the community and a sad blow to those near and dear to him.

The funeral took place on Thursday, and throughout the town there were many tokens of mourning. Flags were flying half mast on numerous buildings, and the windows of tradesmen's shops and of large numbers of private houses. were darkened. Shortly before one o'clock the procession left The Mount. In front walked mem- bers of theßoard of Commissioners, the School Board, Burial Board, tradesmen, friends, and the representa- tives of commercial undertakings, from far and near,the office staff of the firm' the workmen and the employes en the Aston estate. Following was the doctor's carri- age. containing Dr. McDougall and the Rev. Thomas Wilkes (Superintendent Wesleyan minister), and then all eyes were turned on the hearse, through the open sides of which could be seen the handsome oak coffin, with' its panels of silver furniture. the lid being hidden beneath beautiful floral emblems. The roof of the hearse was one mass of lovely bloom, formed into wreaths, crosses, anchors, and other symbols. Following were tour coaches. In the first were Mr. Philip Speakman, J.P., CC. (father) and Messrs. Ernest, Eaton, and Charley Speakman (brothers) ; in the second coach were Messrs. J. J. Speakman and Aoraham Speak- man (uncles), Thomas Wright (Halton) and Themes Lewis; in the third, Messrs. C. Baguley, J. Rothwell, W. Read, and James Ward; and in the fourth, Messrs. Vietor Douetil (brother-in-law), W. A. Stearns, C. B. Barlow' and J. R. Wylde. Then came the private car- riage of Mr. Fairhurst senior, «of Warringten, by whom it was occupied. He was accompanied by his son Mr. F. Fairhurst. The rear was brought up by Mr. Speakman's closed carriage drawn by a pair of horses. On arriving at St. Michael's church, the cortege was met at the en- trance gate by the Vicar,the Rev. Canon Maitland Wood, and the Revs. aE. Seeley, A. J. Jameson and G. F. Montgomery together with the surpliced choir and Mr. W. Cropper, churchwarde n,and Messrs. Peacock, Wes- ton, Sherlock, Clark, and Blundell. sidemen. The coffin was borne into the sacred edifice on the shoulders of four old servants, namely, Means. U. Caunce, .1, Clarke, W. Parker, and J. Barker; and preceding,walked the clergy and choir, solemnly chanting the portion of Scripture commencing "I am the resurrection and the life, saith the Lord." A crowd of nearly a thousand watched these proceedings from Greenway-road, and a large congre- gation assembled in the church, „where the service was impressively spoken by the Rev: A. J. Jameson. The hymn" Let the sons on earth in concert sing" was sung, and also " Onward Christian Sildiers," as a recessional hymn. At the conclusion of this service, the procession re-formed and proceeded to the graveside, where the - Vicar performed the last sad rites, and the choir sang "Now the labouree's task is o'er." , Amongst those noticed in the procession were Messrs. MR. GLADSTONE'S BIRTHDAY.

Mr. Gladstone yesterday completed his eighty-fourth year, having been born in Liverpool on Dec. 29, 1809. The executive of the Midlothian Liberal Association has • forwarded to the Premier a resolution in which they congratulate him on attaining, another anniversary of his birthday. They express their obligation to him for representing the county, and refer to the highly distin- guished Ministry he formed. The resolution expressed their warmest wishes that Mr. Gladstone may for many years occupy his high position and pass measures for the public good. There are two older members of the House of Commons, Mr. C. P. Villiers, who was born in 1802, and is therefore ninety-one years of age, and Sir Isaac Holden, who was born in 1107. and is now eighty-six. Of these three. Mr. Gladstone was the first to enter Parliament. He was returned for Newark in 1832,, and in 1834 took office as a Junior Lord of the Tr‘asury. Downing-street presented far more than its usual animation yesterday, on ths anniversary of the Premier's birthday. Not only was the Mail bag an exceptionally heavy one, but telegraph messengers, frequently with batches f telegrams, succeeded each other with bewildering rapidity in their journeys to Mr. Gladstone's house. The Central News is informed that among those who have sent their con- gratulations are the Queen, the Prince and Prin- cess of %Vales, anti tha prominent stat,,sinen of both , political parties. Mr. Gladstone has also received many touching messages from America and the Continent, several European political leaders being amongst those who have sent their hearty good wishes. The Premier rose early yesterday morning, and in the best of health Be attended to his voluminous correspondence after breakfast, and met his colleagues at noon in the Council 'Chamber, when he was profoundly affected by the warmth with which he was greeted. Mr. &ladstone, accompanied by Mrs. Gladstone, went for a drive in the park in an open carriage before going down to the House, and on their arrival in Palace-yard they were loudly cheered by a large crowd who had awaited their appearance. Both looked quite well. OVATION IN THE HOUSE OF COMMONS.

Mr. Gladstone entered the house during question time, looking extremely well and wearing a rose sur- rounded with lilies of the valley in his coat. The appear- ance of the righ:: hon. gentleman was the signal for a hearty demonstration, all the Ministerialists and Irish Nationalists rising in their places attA 0112trittg loudly, 1

PROPOSED NEW AB ATTOI R FOR . ST.

HELENS.

-- Dr. Robertson, assistant medical officer. reported to the St. Helens Health Committee on Wednesday on the slaughter-house accommodation of the borough. Daring the year there had been an increase in the number of animals killed in the Corporation Abattoir, which. caused great incon- venience to butchers, many of whom nail to kill during the night. In 1892 tbe number killed via 959" while up to the present in 1893 there had been 1,325 beasts killed. Durimr the last five weeks the numbers had been 50, 38,3. 49 and 82 respect' YelY• In order to remedy this tempmarily. in. Al... The building would be central tor the butchers, and it would be away from any diwelling-house,—The Chairman (Counipllor Forster) thought the best plam would be to take Bailey's premises, and instruct the surveyor to prepare plans for the erection of a new building. Dr. Robertson said they had a certain number of men licensed to kill at the Cor- poration Slaughter-housa, and they were killing more beasts than there was accommodation for. He thought the tendency in St. Helens was to have home-killed meat.—Aiderrnan J. C. Gamble said the town was increasing, and they would have to do somethilig.—lt was decided to take the premises of Mr. Bailey for twelve months, and the surveyor was instructed to Prepare plans for a new building.

THINGS HEARD ,AND SEEN AT RUNCORN. • Br AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT. That the weather during Christmastide has been more like spring than winter. That on Christmas m.)rning the atmosphere WU blight and crisp, and a stroll was Car wing. . That a change came in the afternoon, wien there was half a kale bloring accompanied with heavy rain. That despite the bad time most tradesmen admit to having had, there was quite an average amount of business. That the Tonic Sol-kfa Cooir Cliristuaas concert is becoming a popular institution. It was pleasing to see the hall well filled and to think that the success from a musical point of view will bo accompanied by success financially. That one of the saddest occurrences of the week has been the death ot Mr. Tom Speakuian after a prolonged and painful illness. That universal sympathy is extended to the be- reaved parents in their deep sorrow. That the Gilchrist lectures were a splendid suc- cess all round. There is a balance in hand gf .£2 16s. That everybody will agree that the lectures have greatly advanced the cause of the Technical Insti- tute, and the Gilchrist Ti ustees have been informed of that fact and cordially thanked for providing the course of lectures. • That it bas been decided that the old. folks treat 'at Widnes shall be held on January lith, it con- venient to the Mayor.
